# Collected Illustrations of the Three Realms <note>original: "三才圖會" (Sancai Tuhui)</note>

The cover of this traditional Chinese thread-bound book is crafted from deep indigo paper. Affixed to the left side is a vertical white title strip bearing four large characters written in a formal <term>Regular Script<note>Known as Kaishu, this is the standard, clear calligraphy style used for printing and official documents.</note></term>. On the right edge, the white binding thread is visible, displaying a classic <term>four-hole stitching pattern<note>A traditional East Asian method of bookbinding where loose leaves are sewn together through punched holes near the spine.</note></term> characteristic of late Ming Dynasty publishing.

<summary>This is the title page and cover of the "Sancai Tuhui," a massive illustrated encyclopedia compiled by Wang Qi and Wang Siyi in 1609. The title refers to the "Three Realms" of Heaven, Earth, and Humanity, which the work seeks to categorize and illustrate comprehensively.</summary>

<keywords>Sancai Tuhui, Ming Dynasty, encyclopedia, Chinese bookbinding, Three Realms, Regular Script, Wang Qi</keywords>

Corrected by his son, Wang Siyi

# The Seven Mansions of the South <note>Refers to the Southern Palace, one of the four major divisions of the Chinese sky, associated with the Vermilion Bird and the season of summer.</note>

->**Diagram of the Southern Sky**<-

The constellations depicted here represent the stars visible in the southern quadrant of the sky. In traditional Chinese cosmology, these stars were not merely distant suns but celestial officials, seasonal markers, and reflections of the earthly imperial court.

### The Lunar Mansions of the Vermilion Bird

The primary mansions (zodiacal regions) shown in this section include:

*   **The Well** <note>original: "井" (Jing); corresponds roughly to the constellation Gemini.</note>: Considered the head of the Vermilion Bird, governing water and domestic affairs.
*   **The Ghost** <note>original: "鬼" (Gui); corresponds to the constellation Cancer.</note>: Associated with the spirits of the departed and the management of ancestral rites.

### Stars of the Upper and Middle Reaches

*   **Accumulated Water** <note>original: "積水" (Jishui).</note>
*   **The Northern River** <note>original: "北河" (Beihe).</note>
*   **The Pile of Firewood** <note>original: "積薪" (Jixin).</note>
*   **The Water Level** <note>original: "水位" (Shuiwei).</note>
*   **The Celestial Balance** <note>original: "天衡" (Tianheng).</note>
*   **The Beacon Fire** <note>original: "爟" (Guan).</note>
*   **The Outer Kitchen** <note>original: "外廚" (Waichu).</note>
*   **The Three Stars** <note>original: "參" (Shen); while usually part of the Western Palace, it is depicted here due to its proximity to the southern horizon. It corresponds to the belt of Orion.</note>

### Stars of the Deep South

*   **The Palace Gates Mound** <note>original: "闕丘" (Queqiu).</note>
*   **The Wolf** <note>original: "狼" (Lang); known in the West as Sirius, the brightest star in the night sky. In Chinese astrology, its brightness and color were used to predict invasions or thefts.</note>
*   **The Bow and Arrow** <note>original: "弧矢" (Hushi); aimed directly at the Wolf star to keep it in check.</note>
*   **The Wild Pheasant** <note>original: "野雞" (Yeji).</note>
*   **The Military Market** <note>original: "軍市" (Junshi).</note>
*   **The Old Man, the Son, and the Grandson** <note>original: "丈人, 子, 孫" (Zhangren, Zi, Sun).</note>

->**The Old Man of the South Pole**<-
<note>original: "南極老人" (Nanji Laoren); known in the West as Canopus. This star is the second brightest in the sky. In Chinese tradition, its appearance was a rare and auspicious omen, symbolizing national peace and the longevity of the Emperor.</note>

***

<summary>This page provides a star chart and labels for the Seven Mansions of the South, part of the Vermilion Bird constellation group. It identifies key stars like Sirius (The Wolf) and Canopus (The Old Man of the South Pole) which held significant symbolic value in ancient Chinese statecraft and folklore.</summary>
<keywords>Seven Mansions of the South, Vermilion Bird, Sirius, Canopus, Jing Mansion, Gui Mansion, Sancai Tuhui, Chinese Astronomy</keywords>

# The Well Mansion (24 Degrees)

Master Gan says: The eight stars of the **Well** are located within the River <note>original: "河中" - referring to the Heavenly River, or the Milky Way</note>. They govern wells and springs. The Sun, Moon, and the five planets pass through them, making this the "Middle Path" <note>a reference to the Ecliptic, the apparent path of the Sun across the sky</note>.

Master Shi calls it the **Eastern Well**, and also the **Celestial Well**. It governs the positions of the feudal lords, imperial relatives, and the <term>Three Ducal Ministers<note>The San Gong, the highest-ranking civil officials in the imperial government.</note></term>. Therefore, if these stars are bright and large, it signifies the enfeoffment of lords and the founding of states.

If they tremble or lose their color, it portends the execution of lords and relatives, the deposing and slaughter of the Three Ducal Ministers, and calamity for the Imperial Preceptors.

Zhang Heng says: This is the Southern Gate of Heaven. It is the area traversed by the **Ecliptic**. It serves as the pavilion-watch of the heavens and governs the affairs of the <term>Superintendent of Waters<note>Shuiheng; an official post managing royal parks, granaries, and the coinage of money.</note></term>. It is the source from which the equity of laws and regulations is derived. When a Ruler applies the law with fairness, the stars of the Well will be bright and appear in an orderly alignment. When the Moon lodges within this mansion, there will be responses of wind and rain.

It is also said: The Well is the treasury of the Son of Heaven. <note>The text here is slightly obscured or contains rare technical terms regarding astronomical measurements.</note> If the Sun or Moon are eclipsed here, or if the five planets retrograde and "infringe" upon the mansion, high officials will plot rebellion and war will arise.

The inner six stars should not be overly bright; if they appear too bright, there will be disasters involving water.

The star **Yue** <note>the Battle-Axe</note> is attached to the front of the Well. it governs the monitoring of luxury and debauchery and their subsequent punishment. Therefore, it is undesirable for this star to be as bright or as large as the stars of the Well itself. If it trembles, the Son of Heaven will "use the axe" upon his high officials <note>meaning he will carry out executions of high-ranking members of the court</note>.

The three stars of the **South River** and the three stars of the **North River** are divided...

<summary>This page describes the astrological significance of the Well (Jing) lunar mansion, detailing its governance over imperial officials, legal fairness, and natural disasters. It emphasizes how the brightness and movement of these stars serve as omens for the stability of the state and the conduct of the Emperor.</summary>

<keywords>Jing Mansion, Well Mansion, Gan De, Shi Shen, Zhang Heng, Ecliptic, Three Ducal Ministers, Battle-Axe star, Chinese Astronomy, Astrological Omens</keywords>

# The Eastern Well (Continued)

->Flanking the **Eastern Well** is the "Heaven’s Height"<- <note>original: "天高" (Tiangao). This refers to a high point in the celestial sphere or a specific gate</note>. This is the Lookout Tower Gate of Heaven. It governs the security of passes and bridges. To the south of the River <note>The Milky Way</note> is the **Southern Limit**, or the Southern Boundary. It is also called the Southern Palace, the Gate of Yang, the Gate of Yue, or the Star of Power. It governs the element of Fire.

The **Northern River** is called the **Northern Limit**, or the Northern Boundary. <gloss>It is also called</gloss> the Gate of Yin, the Gate of the Hu, or the Star of the Balance. It governs the element of Water.

The space between these **Two Limits** is the constant path of the **Three Lights** <term>Three Lights<note>A collective term for the Sun, Moon, and the five visible planets (Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn).</note></term>. The Southern Limit serves to mark the borders of the *Man* and *Yi* peoples; the Northern Limit serves to mark the borders of the *Rong* and *Di* peoples <note>These were the traditional terms for non-Chinese ethnic groups living to the south/east and west/north of the central Chinese heartland</note>.

The *Classic of Stars* says: The six stars of the **Two Rivers** <note>referring to the North River (Beihe) and South River (Nanhe) asterisms</note> serve as the boundaries for passes and bridges; they identify the wicked and the deceitful. If the southern stars are not visible, then the southern roads are blocked. If the northern stars are not visible, then the northern roads are obstructed. If the River Limits tremble or shake, then war will arise in the Middle Kingdom.

The three stars of the **Celestial Wine-Cup** <note>original: "天罇" (Tianzun)</note> are located to the south of the **Five Feudal Lords**. They govern the filling of grain and porridge to ensure the proper supply of wine and food. **Zhang Heng** says: They provide for the poor and the hungry. In divination, if they are bright, the year will be bountiful; if they are dim, the year will be one of famine. Or, some say that their being dim is auspicious.

The five stars of the **Five Feudal Lords** <note>original: "五諸侯" (Wu Zhuhou)</note> are located to the northeast of the **Well**, near the **Northern River**. They govern the investigation and reporting of official misconduct and guard against the unforeseen. It is also said: they govern the balance of *Yin* and *Yang* and observe the successes and failures of the state. They are also called...

<summary>This page describes the celestial "limits" flanking the Milky Way, which serve as astrological indicators for border security and the movement of the Sun and Moon. It also details the stars governing food supplies and the oversight of government officials.</summary>

<keywords>Southern Limit, Northern Limit, Three Lights, Celestial Wine-Cup, Five Feudal Lords, Zhang Heng, border security, famine divination</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed description of the Eastern Well (Dongjing) lunar mansion, specifically focusing on the stars that symbolize the imperial court’s inner circle and the celestial officials in charge of water management.</meta>

### The Imperial Attendant Stars

The primary **Emperor Stars** are five in number. The first is called the **Emperor’s Teacher**, or sometimes the **Emperor’s Heart**. The second is the **Emperor’s Friend**. The third is the **Three Dukes** <note>The highest-ranking officials in the ancient Chinese government</note>. The fourth is the **Erudite** <note>A scholar-official or 'Doctor' of the classics</note>. The fifth is the **Grand Historian**, also known as the **Five Grand Masters**. These five stars are constant companions to the Emperor, serving to resolve his doubts and guide his deliberations. 

In the practice of divination, if these stars appear bright, large, and "moist" in luster, and if they are equal in size to one another, then the world will be well-governed; this is a great blessing for the state. However, if they appear with sharp rays or "horns" <note>scintillating excessively</note>, it indicates that calamity is brewing within the court. If they are dim or unclear, it suggests that the high and low officials will become suspicious of one another, and loyal, virtuous men will not be employed. **Zhang Heng** <note>The famous Han dynasty astronomer and mathematician (78–139 CE)</note> also stated: "If they appear red, there will be a bountiful harvest; if they are dark, it portends misfortune."

### The Celestial Stores and Water Officials

The star **Accumulated Water** <note>original: 薪積水, likely referring to the star *Jishui*</note> is located to the north of the **North River** asterism. It is responsible for providing the wine used in ceremonies. In divination, if this star cannot be seen, it is a sign of impending disaster. It also serves as a sentinel for observing flood-related calamities.

The star **Accumulated Firewood** <term>Jixin<note>A single star in the Gemini constellation representing the storage of fuel for the imperial kitchens.</note></term> is located to the east of **Accumulated Water**. Its purpose is to provide for the needs of the imperial kitchens. If it shines brightly, the sovereign will enjoy good health; if it is faint, there will be blame and misfortune. If the planet **Mars** <note>original: 火 (Fire Star)</note> "guards" or lingers near this star, a great drought is foretold.

The **Water Treasury** consists of four stars located to the southwest of the **Well** mansion. These are the celestial water officials. Their divinatory significance is the same as that of the **Water Level**.

The **Water Level** consists of four stars to the east of the **Well**. This asterism governs the "Water Balance" <note>an official responsible for canal and dam maintenance</note> and also oversees the discharge, overflow, and flow of currents. Regarding this, the Master **Wu Xian** <note>A legendary astronomer of the Shang Dynasty</note> offered this praise: 

> "The four stars of the **Water Level** govern discharge and overflow. If they shift their position to move close to the **North River**, then the kingdom will be submerged and turned into rivers and streams, or suffer the duality of flood and fire."

<summary>This page describes the astrological significance of stars near the Eastern Well, categorizing them into imperial advisors and officials governing water and resources.</summary>
<keywords>Emperor Stars, Zhang Heng, Accumulated Water (Jishui), Accumulated Firewood (Jixin), Water Treasury (Shuifu), Water Level (Shuiwei), Wu Xian, celestial omens</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the astrological survey of the Eastern Well (Dongjing) mansion, transitioning from the celestial water officials to the asterisms representing the earthly rivers, the military supply lines, and the lineage of the imperial household.</meta>

...and if a **Guest Star** <term>Kexing: a temporary celestial object such as a nova or comet</term> lingers within or encroaches upon these stars, the "hundred rivers" will overflow their banks.

### The Four Waterways and the Military Market

The **Four Channels** <note>original: 四瀆, Sidu; representing the four great rivers of ancient China: the Yangtze, Yellow, Huai, and Ji</note> consist of four stars located to the south of the **Well** mansion and east of **Xuanyuan** <note>The constellation Leo</note>—though some records place them to the west of Xuanyuan. They are the celestial essence of the Yangtze, Yellow, Huai, and Ji rivers. In the practice of divination, if these stars appear bright and large, it indicates that the earthly waters will rise and flood.

The **Military Market** <note>original: 軍市, Junshi</note> is a group of thirteen stars arranged in a circular shape like a string of coins. They are located southeast of the constellation **Orion** <note>original: 參, Shen</note>, or according to some, south of the **South River**. This asterism represents the marketplace where the Heavenly Army trades its goods. If a **Guest Star** enters this area, or if the **Metal Star** <term>Venus</term> or the **Fire Star** <term>Mars</term> lingers there, the army will suffer a great famine. Some say that if these stars appear to shake or change their color, it also portends a great famine for the troops.

The **Wild Pheasant** <note>original: 野雞, Yeji</note> is a single star located in the center of the Military Market. It governs strange omens and supernatural transformations. In divination, if it appears blurry with sharp rays <note>original: 茫角, mangjiao; indicating excessive twinkling</note> or seems to tremble, it is a sign of impending military disaster. If the star moves out of its usual position, it indicates that the regional lords will raise their armies in rebellion.

### The Ancestral Lineage and the Palace Gates

To the southwest of the Military Market are two stars called the **Old Man** <note>original: 丈人, Zhangren</note>. To the east of the Old Man are two stars called the **Son** <note>original: 子, Zi</note>, and to the east of the Son are two stars called the **Grandson** <note>original: 孫, Sun</note>. 

The **Old Man** governs the elderly and venerable ministers of the state. If these stars cannot be seen, it signifies that the ministers are being blocked and cannot communicate with the Emperor. The **Son** and **Grandson** stars are positioned to "attend" at the side of the Old Man, dwelling together in a supportive manner. If they become invisible, it portends a disaster for the lineage; however, as long as they maintain their constant, normal appearance, there is no cause for blame.

The **Palace Gates** <note>original: 闕丘, Queqiu</note> are three stars located to the southeast of the South River. They represent the ceremonial gate towers of the Emperor's palace and the dual watchtowers of the regional lords' estates. If **Venus** or **Mars** lingers within this asterism, it is a sign of impending battle at the very gates of the palace. 

The **Wolf** <note>original: 狼, Lang; the star Sirius</note>...

<summary>This page details the astrology of stars representing China's four major rivers, the military's logistical markets, and the symbolic representation of family lineage and palace security in the night sky.</summary>
<keywords>Four Channels (Sidu), Military Market (Junshi), Wild Pheasant star, Old Man/Son/Grandson stars, Palace Gates (Queqiu), Guest Stars, planetary omens</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the astrological descriptions of stars near the Eastern Well mansion, specifically focusing on the martial "Wild General," the "Bow and Arrow" used for defense, and the auspicious "Old Man Star" of longevity.</meta>

### The Wild General and the Heavenly Bow

One star, located to the southeast of the **Well** mansion, is large and bright. It is known as the **Wild General** <note>original: 野將, Yejiang; possibly the star Beta Canis Majoris or a similar star within the Military Market asterism</note>. It governs matters of killing and looting. Its color should remain constant; it is undesirable for it to change its appearance or shift its position.

If this star wavers or shakes, it portends the rise of bandits and the rebellion of **foreign troops** <note>original: 胡兵, Hubing; historically referring to nomadic groups from the north or west</note>, leading to times so desperate that people resort to cannibalism. If the star appears agitated, the sovereign will find no peace and will be forced to gallop restlessly across the empire.

**Zhang Heng** <note>78–139 CE; a renowned Chinese polymath, astronomer, and inventor</note> says: "If the star dwells out of its proper place, people will eat one another. If its color is yellow-white and bright, it is a sign of good fortune. Black is ominous. If it appears red with sharp, scintillating rays <note>original: 茫角, mangjiao</note>, war will break out. If the **Metal Star** <term>Venus</term> or the **Fire Star** <term>Mars</term> lingers near it, the omens are the same."

The **Bow and Arrow** <note>original: 弧矢, Hushi; an asterism in the constellations Canis Major and Puppis</note> consists of nine stars located to the southeast of the **Wolf** <note>The star Sirius</note>. It represents the "Heavenly Bow," prepared as a defense against bandits. The bow must always point toward the Wolf.

If the Bow and Arrow shifts or moves irregularly, there will be many bandits. If it appears exceptionally bright, a great army will arise. When both the Wolf and the Bow and Arrow are prominently visible and "tense," the empire is on the verge of chaos. It is further said: "When the Heavenly Bow is drawn tight, the entire world will be consumed by war, and the ruler and his ministers will plot against one another." Zhang Heng also notes: "If the bow appears drawn to its full extent, war will break out across the realm."

### The Star of Longevity

The **Old Man** <note>original: 老人星, Laorenxing; the star Canopus</note> is a single star located to the south of the Bow. It is also known as the **South Pole** <note>original: 南極, Nanji; not the geographic pole, but the southern celestial pole star of the Chinese system</note>. This star governs longevity and old age. 

It typically becomes visible in the direction of **Bing** <note>South-Southeast</note> on the day of the **Spring Equinox** <term>the moment in March when day and night are of equal length</term>. On the evening of the Spring Equinox, it sets in the direction of **Ding** <note>South-Southwest</note>. On the evening of the **Autumn Equinox** <term>the equivalent moment in September</term>, it appears again at the position of *Ding*. To observe it, one should go to the southern suburbs of the city.

If this star appears large and bright, the sovereign will enjoy a long life and the empire will be peaceful and serene. However, if the star is not visible when it should be, it is a cause for great concern for the sovereign.

<summary>This page describes the astrological significance of the Wild General and Bow and Arrow stars as omens of war and banditry, contrasted with the Old Man star (Canopus) as a symbol of peace and imperial longevity.</summary>
<keywords>Wild General, Bow and Arrow, Sirius (Wolf), Canopus (Old Man Star), Zhang Heng, military omens, longevity, Spring Equinox, Autumn Equinox</keywords>

## Celestial Map of the Ghost and Willow Mansions

->**North**<-

->**The Star of Severed Remains**<-
-><note>original: 星斬骸, Xingzhanhai; an asterism associated with the punishment of criminals and the aftermath of battle, reflecting the somber nature of the adjacent Ghost mansion</note><-

**East** <note>The map is oriented with East on the left, following traditional Chinese celestial charts which mirror the sky as seen from below.</note>

**The Willow**
<note>original: 柳, Liu; the 24th of the twenty-eight Lunar Mansions. It represents the beak of the Vermilion Bird of the South and governs the ripening of vegetation and the management of kitchens.</note>

<note>original: 外廚, Waichu; a grouping of stars in the constellation Hydra, responsible for the "pure food" used in state sacrifices to the ancestors.</note>

<note>original: 天紀, Tianji; representing the laws, rules, and social order that bind the empire together.</note>

<note>original: 積尸氣, Jishiqi; known today as the Beehive Cluster (M44). Ancient astronomers saw this hazy nebula as a cloud of ethereal vapors rising from the spirits of the dead.</note>

-> ### Collected Illustrations of the Three Realms, Volume 2: Astronomy <-

### The Ghost Mansion

The **Ghost Mansion** <note>original: 輿鬼 (Yugui). The 23rd of the 28 Lunar Mansions, corresponding to the constellation Cancer.</note> spans two degrees and serves as the central path for the Sun, Moon, and the Five Planets. It governs matters of death, misfortune, and disease. **Zhang Heng** <note>A renowned Han Dynasty astronomer and polymath (78–139 CE).</note> states that it presides over ancestral sacrifices and is considered the **"Eye of Heaven."** It also oversees keen observation, the detection of treacherous plots, and the carrying out of executions.

*   The **Northeast star** governs the accumulation of horses.
*   The **Southeast star** governs the accumulation of weaponry and soldiers.
*   The **Southwest star** governs the accumulation of cloth and silk.
*   The **Northwest star** governs the accumulation of gold and jade.

Divination is performed by observing the changes in these four stars. 

The single star in the center is named the **Cumulative Corpse-Gas** <note>original: 積尸氣 (Jishiqi). This refers to the Beehive Cluster (Messier 44). To the naked eye, it appears as a faint, cloudy nebula.</note>. One interpretation says it represents a heap of corpses where only the "vapor" is visible; it governs death, mourning, and sacrificial rites. Another interpretation calls it the **Iron Lock**, governing executions; it is also known as the **Ghost’s Execution Block Star**.

When the stars of the Ghost Mansion are bright, the "five grains" <note>A traditional grouping of staple crops, signifying a successful harvest.</note> will ripen. If they are dim, the populace will scatter. If they flicker and shine intensely, taxes and forced labor will increase. If the stars shift their positions, the people will be filled with sorrow and government decrees will become harsh and urgent. For the Ghost Mansion, it is actually a sign of good fortune to be hazy and dim. If it appears bright, war will break out, high ministers will conspire against the throne, and the people will become refugees—a dire omen.

**Master Gan** <note>Gan De, a famous astronomer from the 4th century BCE.</note> says: If the "Cumulative Corpse-Gas" flickers or loses its color, there will be epidemics, ghosts will be heard wailing, and the land will fall into famine.

The four stars to the west of **Xuanyuan** <note>A constellation associated with the Yellow Emperor, containing the star Regulus.</note> are called the **Beacons**, also known as the **Signal Fires**. These represent the lighting of fires to warn of emergencies. In divination, it is a favorable sign when they are dim and still. If they appear large and bright, then...

<summary>This page describes the astrological significance of the Ghost Mansion (Cancer) and the Beehive Cluster, detailing how their appearance predicts everything from economic prosperity to war and famine. It emphasizes that for this specific "ghostly" constellation, a dim appearance is a positive omen for the state.</summary>

<keywords>Ghost Mansion, Yugui, Beehive Cluster, Jishiqi, Zhang Heng, Gan De, Chinese Astrology, Lunar Mansions, Divination, Omens</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the astrological descriptions of constellations near the Ghost Mansion, specifically detailing the Heavenly Dog, the Outer Kitchen, the Celestial Altar, and the Celestial Record stars, along with their divinatory significance for the state and the natural world.</meta>

...the frontier borders will face urgent danger. If the stars flicker or appear to have pointed rays, the omen is the same. It is also said that if they are bright, it is auspicious; if they are dim, it is ill-omened.

### The Heavenly Dog
The **Heavenly Dog** <note>original: 天狗 (Tiangou). A seven-star asterism located in the modern constellations of Pyxis and Vela.</note>, consisting of seven stars, is located to the southwest of the **Ghost Mansion** and the northeast of the **Wolf Star** <note>original: 狼 (Lang). Known in the West as Sirius.</note>. It sits within the **Horizontal River** <note>original: 橫河 (Henghe). A reference to the Milky Way.</note> to guard against thieves. In <term>divination</term> <note>original: 占 (zhan). The practice of interpreting celestial omens.</note>, if these stars shift or move, bandits and thieves will arise. If **Venus** <note>original: 金 (Jin, the Metal Planet).</note> or **Mars** <note>original: 火 (Huo, the Fire Planet).</note> "encroaches" upon them, it foretells such severe famine or war that people will resort to cannibalism.

### The Outer Kitchen
The **Outer Kitchen** <note>original: 外厨 (Waichu).</note>, consisting of six stars, is located to the south of the **Willow Mansion** <note>original: 柳 (Liu). The 24th Lunar Mansion.</note>. It serves as the "outer kitchen" for the **Son of Heaven** <note>The Emperor.</note>. The divinatory interpretations for this constellation are the same as those for the **Celestial Kitchen** <note>original: 天厨 (Tianchu). A constellation in Draco responsible for the imperial dietary needs.</note>.

### The Celestial Altar
To the south of the **Bow** <note>original: 弧 (Hu). An asterism in Canis Major and Puppis.</note> are the six stars of the **Celestial Altar** <note>original: 天社 (Tianshe). Located in the constellation Vela.</note>, situated to the southeast of the **Old Man Star** <note>original: 老人 (Laoren). Known in the West as Canopus.</note>.
*   If the stars are straight and bright, it is auspicious, and the **Altars of Soil and Grain** <note>original: 社稷 (Sheji). A metaphor for the state itself.</note> will be peaceful.
*   If they flicker or shake, officials are plotting against the sovereign.
*   If they are slightly dim, the state is in peril.

The **Treatise of the Sui Dynasty** <note>original: 隋志 (Sui Zhi). Referring to the "Treatise on Astronomy" within the *Book of Sui*.</note> states that **Goulóng** <note>original: 句龍.</note>, the son of the deity **Gònggōng**, was able to level the waters and the earth; therefore, he is sacrificed to alongside the Altar of the Soil. His spirit became this constellation.

### The Celestial Record
To the south of the **Outer Kitchen** is a single star called the **Celestial Record** <note>original: 天紀 (Tianji).</note>. It governs the knowledge of the "teeth and years" <note>An ancient way of referring to the age and lifespan.</note> of birds and beasts. If Venus or Mars "guards" this star <note>i.e., lingers near it in retrograde or conjunction.</note>, or if the star becomes invisible, then many birds and beasts will die.

<summary>This page details the astrological significance of constellations near the Ghost Mansion, linking celestial movements to earthly events like banditry, imperial stability, and the health of wildlife.</summary>
<keywords>Heavenly Dog, Outer Kitchen, Celestial Altar, Goulóng, Celestial Record, star omens, Book of Sui, Sirius</keywords>

**The Well** <note>original: 井 (Jǐng). The 22nd Lunar Mansion, corresponding to the constellation Gemini. It is traditionally associated with water, springs, and the prevention of fire.</note>
<column-break/>
**The Willow** <note>original: 柳 (Liǔ). The 24th Lunar Mansion, making up the "beak" or "neck" of the Vermilion Bird. It is thought to govern the kitchen and royal banquets.</note>

**The Yellow Emperor** <note>original: 軒轅 (Xuānyuán). A large constellation consisting of seventeen stars in the modern constellations of Leo and Lynx. It is named after the legendary sovereign and represents the authority of the Imperial Court. Its brightest star is Regulus.</note>

**Banner of Wine** <note>original: 酒旗 (Jiǔqí). A group of three stars representing the flag of a tavern. In <term>astromancy</term>, it governs the preparation of wine and seasonal feasts.</note>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the 28 Lunar Mansions, specifically moving to the Willow mansion (Liu), which follows the Ghost mansion discussed previously. The text maintains the standard format of citing ancient authorities like Gan De and Zhang Heng to explain the astrological significance of celestial bodies.</meta>

The Willow mansion <note>original: "柳" (Liǔ)</note> spans fourteen degrees and is designated as the Middle Path of Heaven. Master Gan <note>Gan De, a pioneering Chinese astronomer from the 4th century BCE</note> states that it governs the positions of food, drink, granaries, warehouses, wine, and vinegar.

When these stars appear **bright and large**, the people will enjoy an abundance of food and wine. If they are seen to **tremble or shift**, it foretells the death of a high official or nobleman due to excessive drinking. If the stars **lose their luster**, the realm will be restless, and famine will force the people to wander the roads; these consequences are certain to manifest within no more than three years.

Zhang Heng <note>A celebrated polymath and astronomer of the Han Dynasty (78–139 CE)</note> explains that Willow forms the **crop** <note>The throat or food-storage pouch</note> of the Vermilion Bird <note>The symbol of the southern quadrant of the sky</note>; it is also known as the "Steward of the Celestial Kitchen." 

It presides over the imperial meals and the balancing of flavors. The *Treatise of the Sui Dynasty* <note>Referring to the astronomical records in the Book of Sui (636 CE)</note> notes that it also rules over thunder and rain. It is variously called the "Celestial Prime Minister" or the "Celestial Storehouse," and it also governs the growth of vegetation. 

When the stars are bright:
*   The Great Ministers will act with gravity and caution.
*   The nation will remain in peace.
*   The kitchens will be fully provisioned.
*   The King’s mandate will flourish and wise advisors will come forward.

If the stars appear in a **straight line**, there will be conspiracies throughout the realm to attack the ruler. If they appear **clustered together**, it is an omen of soldiers fighting at the gates of the capital.

The three stars of the **Wine Flag** <note>original: "酒旗" (Jiǔqí)</note> are located to the south of the "Right Horn" of Xuanyuan <note>A large constellation associated with the Yellow Emperor, roughly corresponding to the modern constellation Leo</note>; this is the office of the celestial Wine Official. 

It presides over banquets, food, and drink. When the five planets linger near the Flag, the Emperor will decree a grand festival for the whole world, bestowing gifts of wine, food, and valuables, and granting titles of nobility to the imperial family. The divination states that if these stars shine brightly, there will be many <unclear>rituals</unclear> <unclear>errors</unclear> <unclear>calculations</unclear>.

<summary>This page describes the Willow lunar mansion and the Wine Flag constellation, detailing their roles as celestial providers of food, drink, and rain. The text outlines how the brightness and alignment of these stars predict national stability, imperial banquets, or potential famine and rebellion.</summary>

<keywords>Willow mansion, Wine Flag, Zhang Heng, Gan De, Vermilion Bird, celestial kitchen, astrology, famine omens, Xuanyuan</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the astronomical and astrological descriptions of the lunar mansions and constellations, specifically focusing on the 'Star' lunar mansion and the Xuanyuan constellation (Leo).</meta>

The **Seven Stars** <note>original: 星七度 (Xing Qi Du); this refers to the 'Star' lunar mansion, which forms part of the modern constellation Hydra</note> span seven degrees. Master Gan <note>Gan De, a pioneering 4th-century BCE Chinese astronomer</note> states that these stars govern the positions of the Empress and the concubines of the inner palace; they also represent virtuous scholars. If these stars lose their color or flicker and shift, it foretells the death of the Empress or the execution of virtuous scholars. When they are bright and large, the "Way" <note>the Dao, or the natural moral order</note> is fulfilled and its transformative power is realized.

Zhang Heng <note>a renowned scientist and polymath of the Han Dynasty</note> says the Seven Stars form the neck of the <term>Vermilion Bird</term> <note>one of the Four Symbols of the Chinese constellations, representing the South and the season of summer</note>. Some call the "Neck" the "Heavenly Capital," as it rules over clothing, embroidered patterns, and fine silks.

The *Treatise of the Sui Dynasty* <note>the Sui Shu, an official history book</note> notes that they rule over urgent military deployments, defensive guards, and the capture of thieves. They also represent virtuous officials tasked with emergency matters. Therefore, it is desirable for these stars to be bright, which signifies that the <term>King’s Way</term> <note>the ideal of benevolent and righteous rule</note> is flourishing. If they are dim, the virtuous and talented are not employed, the empire becomes hollow, and the <term>Son of Heaven</term> <note>the Emperor</note> falls ill. If the stars move, war arises; if they shift from their proper place, the government will be overthrown.

If the stars lose their color or appear hazy and pointed, the Empress will die and virtuous ministers will be executed. A solar eclipse in this region signifies war, famine, and disasters for women. If the Wood Star <note>Jupiter</note> encroaches upon them, the people will be at peace. If the Fire Star <note>Mars</note> encroaches, there will be drought. If the Metal, Earth, or Water stars <note>Venus, Saturn, and Mercury</note> encroach, all bring calamity. Lunar halos or "bushy" comets <note>original: 孛 (bei); referring to comets with a fuzzy or diffuse appearance</note> appearing here signify the start of war.

**Xuanyuan** <note>a constellation comprising seventeen stars, including the bright star Regulus in the modern constellation Leo</note> consists of seventeen stars located to the north of the Seven Stars. It represents the spirit of the **Yellow Emperor** and the body of the **Yellow Dragon**. It also governs the inner palace, specifically the mistress of the Empress and concubines and the duties of the palace women. One name for it is the *Eastern Mound*, and another is the *Weight Star*. It is the god of thunder and rain in the heavens. The large southern star is the symbol of the Empress herself; the next star to the north represents the Imperial Consort and the Screen <note>a protective barrier or a sign of palace privacy</note>.

<summary>This page describes the astrological significance of the 'Star' lunar mansion and the Xuanyuan constellation, linking celestial movements to the fate of the Empress, virtuous officials, and the stability of the imperial government.</summary>
<keywords>Seven Stars, Vermilion Bird, Xuanyuan, Yellow Emperor, Chinese Astrology, Imperial Palace, Empress, Celestial Omens</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the astronomical survey of the constellations, moving from the 'Star' lunar mansion into a detailed description of the Xuanyuan constellation, which corresponds to parts of the modern constellation Leo. It maps the celestial arrangement to the hierarchy of the Imperial Inner Palace.</meta>

[The brightest star is] the **Great General**. The next star to the north is the **Consort**. The one after that is the **General**. The remaining stars following these are categorized as the various **Concubines** and their attendants. 

To the south, the small stars represent the **Waiting Maids**. To the left, one star represents the **Common People** or the **Empress’s Younger Clan**. To the right, one star represents the **Great People** or the **Empress’s Elder Clan**. 

In divination, it is desirable for their color to be bright, lustrous, yellow, and small, which signifies good fortune. **Zhang Heng** <note>78–139 CE; a polymath of the Han Dynasty who improved the armillary sphere</note> says: 

->**Xuanyuan** resembles the body of a dragon.<-

It is the deity governing the spirits of **thunder and rain**, and serves as the celestial image of the **Inner Palace** <note>the section of the palace reserved for the Empress and concubines</note>. When <term>Yin and Yang</term> <note>the two fundamental, opposing but complementary forces of the universe</note> intertwine, their resonance creates thunder; when the resonance is subtle, it becomes lightning. When they are in harmony, it becomes rain; when they are angry, wind; when disordered, fog; when condensed, frost; and when scattered, it becomes dew. Their patterns form the clouds, and when they stand upright, they become rainbows and halos. These fourteen transformations are all governed by Xuanyuan. 

It is desirable for these stars to be small, yellow, and bright, which is auspicious. If they shift from their positions, the people of the nation will wander and be displaced, which is an omen of evil. If they are overly bright and large, there will be strife and rivalry within the Inner Palace. If the eastern and western corners spread out and tremble, the Empress’s clan will meet ruin through their own arrogance and lawlessness. If Jupiter, Mars, or Venus "guard" <note>remain stationary near</note> these stars, it is an ill omen for the Empress. 

The *Book of Han* <note>original: 漢書 (Han Shu); the official history of the Western Han Dynasty</note> states: 

> **Xuanyuan** acts as the weight of the scales, while **Supreme Tenuity** <note>original: 太微 (Taiwei); a major star region representing the celestial court or government</note> acts as the balance beam. 

If the Moon or the Five Planets guard or encroach upon these stars, it is like a scale being perfectly balanced, which is a sign of stability and luck. The four stars of the **Inner Screen** are located in...

It provides astrological omens concerning the Empress and the stability of the court based on the stars' color and position.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the astronomical survey of the constellations near Xuanyuan (the Great Chariot/Leo), focusing on the stars that symbolize judicial fairness, the Prime Minister’s office, and the agricultural health of the empire. ### Collected Illustrations of the Three Realms, Volume 2: Astronomy

The **Middle Terrace** <note>Zhongtai; part of the "Three Terraces" asterism in Ursa Major</note> is located to the north of the **Southern Radiance**. It represents the official of the **Law Enforcer** <note>Zhifa</note> who ensures the fairness of criminal punishments. In divination, if these stars are bright, then punishments and penalties are balanced; if they are dim, they are not.

The **Wine Banner** <note>Jiuqi; an asterism in the modern constellation Leo</note> consists of three stars to the south known as the **Celestial Assistant**, which serves as the symbolic image of the Prime Minister. In divination, if these stars are bright and small, the Minister is virtuous in his governance. If the stars' "horns" <note>the pointed rays or flares emanating from a star</note> appear large, it indicates the Minister is autocratic and oversteps his authority. If the stars shift from their usual positions, it foretells that the Prime Minister will be replaced.

The **Five Stars of Millet** <note>Ji; an asterism representing the god of agriculture</note> are located to the south of the **Star** mansion <note>Qixing; the 25th lunar mansion</note>. They govern **agricultural administration** and are responsible for the stabilization of the prices and growth of the various cereal crops. If these stars appear bright and large, the year will bring a bountiful harvest. If they are not bright, there will be scarcity and need. If they are not visible at all, it is a dire omen of a famine so severe that people will resort to cannibalism.

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the twenty-eight lunar mansions, specifically focusing on the Zhang Mansion (The Spread), its associated star groups, and their astrological significance regarding imperial rituals and national security.</meta>

The <term>Zhang Mansion</term> <note>original: "張宿" (Zhāng Sù); the 26th of the 28 lunar mansions, representing the "spread" of a bird's wings or a net.</note> spans seventeen degrees and constitutes the crop of the <term>Vermilion Bird</term> <note>The celestial quadrant representing the South and the element of Fire.</note>. Master Gan <note>Referring to Gan De, a famous 4th-century BCE astronomer.</note> says that it governs the positions of the Imperial Ancestral Temple, the <term>Hall of Distinction</term> <note>Mingtang; a ritual building where the emperor performed seasonal rites and met with officials.</note>, and the Imperial Censors. It is located upon the Middle Way of Heaven. If the stars are bright and large, the nation is powerful and prosperous; if they lose their color, the Ancestral Temple is uneasy.

The *Astronomical Treatise of the Book of Sui* says that it governs the treasures and clothes used in the Ancestral Temple, as well as the "Heavenly Kitchen" (food and drink) and the granting of rewards. When these stars are bright, the Sovereign performs the Great Suburban Sacrifices <note>Rituals performed at the outskirts of the capital to honor Heaven and Earth.</note>. Because it occupies the center of the heavens, if the stars appear to move or tremble, the distribution of rewards will be shifted or delayed. If rebels gather against the Son of Heaven, there will be military portents; if Venus or Mars "guards" <note>stays stationary within</note> this region, war will break out. Some say it also governs the quality of tribute items. 

If the stars are thin and lack light, the Sovereign will have few descendants. A solar eclipse in this mansion indicates that the ritual codes must be refined and repaired. A lunar eclipse portends major flooding, described as "fish traveling upon the paths of men." If Mars or a comet invades this space, war will arise; if Saturn or Mercury invades, the nation will not be at peace.

South of the Zhang Mansion are fourteen stars called the **Heavenly Temple** <note>Tianmiao</note>; this is the ancestral shrine of the Son of Heaven. If a "guest star" <note>a temporary celestial body like a nova or comet</note> occupies it, the ritual officials will have cause for grief. Its divination is identical to that of the <term>Hollow Beam</term> <note>Xuliang; another star group associated with mourning and temples.</note> constellation.

The **Long Wall** <note>Changyuan</note> consists of four stars located south of the <term>Shaowei</term> <note>The "Lesser Subtlety" stars, representing junior officials or recluses.</note> constellation. It governs the borders and the foreign tribes of the north and west. If Mars occupies it, these tribes will invade the Central Kingdom. If Venus enters it, the Nine Ministers will plot rebellion. 

<summary>This page details the astrological significance of the Zhang Mansion (The Spread), linking its appearance to the health of the Imperial Ancestral Temple, ritual propriety, and the threat of rebellion or foreign invasion.</summary>
<keywords>Zhang Mansion, Vermilion Bird, Imperial Ancestral Temple, Hall of Distinction, Gan De, Book of Sui, omens, solar eclipse, Heavenly Temple, Long Wall constellation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the astronomical discourse from the previous section, moving from the Zhang Mansion (The Spread) to the stars surrounding the Supreme Palace Enclosure, focusing on the celestial representation of government officials and the imperial family.</meta>

# Astronomy

Four stars located to the west of the <term>Supreme Palace Enclosure</term> <note>original: "太微" (Tàiwēi); one of the three "walls" or great enclosures of the Chinese sky, representing the celestial government court.</note>, arranged in a line from north to south, represent the positions of the **scholar-officials**. One name for them is the **Scholar-Recluses**; they are also known as the **Assistant Lords of the Son of Heaven**. Some call them the **Academician Officials**, while others call them the **Balance of Life at the Travelers' Gate**. 

The first star from the south is the **Scholar-Recluse**; the second star is the **Councilor**; the third star is the **Academician**; the fourth star is the **Senior Official**. If these stars appear bright, large, and yellow, then men of worth will be elevated to office. If the Moon or the Five Planets <note>The visible planets: Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> encroach upon or "guard" <note>An astrological term for a planet remaining stationary within or near a star group.</note> them, it portends that the Empress or Empress Dowager will have cause for worry, and the Prime Minister will be replaced.

The <term>Great Venerable</term> <note>original: "太尊" (Tàizūn).</note>, also called the **Heavenly Venerable**, is located within the <term>Terrace</term> <note>Referring to the "Three Terraces" (Sāntái) star group, which served as a celestial ladder or a representation of the three highest cabinet positions.</note>. These stars represent the **imperial relatives**. If they are bright, it is a sign of good fortune; if they are dim, it is an ill omen. If they become invisible, the nation will suffer great sorrow.

<summary>This page describes two star groups—one representing the hierarchy of scholar-officials and the other representing imperial relatives—and outlines how their brightness or celestial movements predict the stability of the government and the royal family.</summary>

<keywords>Supreme Palace Enclosure, Scholar-officials, Scholar-Recluses, Academicians, Great Venerable, Imperial Relatives, Chinese Astrology, Planetary Portents</keywords>

### Collected Illustrations of the Three Realms, Volume 2
## Astronomy

(Page 22)

->**North**<-

# The Supreme Palace Enclosure
<note>original: "太微垣" (Tàiwēi Yuán). One of the "Three Enclosures" of the Chinese sky, this region represents the celestial government office where the Emperor and his ministers handle administrative affairs.</note>

**The Wings**
<note>original: "翼" (Yì). The 27th lunar mansion, representing the wings of the <term>Vermilion Bird</term> <note>The mythological creature representing the South and the season of summer.</note>. It is associated with ritual, music, and foreign envoys.</note>

**The Chariot**
<note>original: "軫" (Zhěn). The 28th and final lunar mansion of the Southern Palace. It symbolizes the celestial carriage and is often associated with military transport and the winds.</note>

**The Horn**
<note>original: "角" (Jiǎo). The 1st lunar mansion of the <term>Azure Dragon</term> <note>The mythological creature representing the East and the season of spring.</note>. As the first mansion of the dragon, it marks the beginning of the celestial cycle and was traditionally used to determine the seasons.</note>

<column-break/>

->**West**<-  **[Star Chart Diagram]**  ->**East**<-

**The Neck**
<note>original: "亢" (Kàng). The 2nd lunar mansion of the Azure Dragon, representing the dragon's throat. In judicial astrology, this area was watched for signs regarding the fairness of trials and the integrity of the court.</note>

**Eastern Ou**
<note>original: "東甌" (Dōng'ōu). An asterism named after a historical kingdom in the region of modern-day Zhejiang. In the heavens, it represents the southern barbarian tribes or the fortification of the southern borders.</note>

->**South**<-

<summary>This page features a star chart from the Sancai Tuhui illustrating the transition between the Southern and Eastern lunar mansions, centered around the Supreme Palace Enclosure. It identifies key constellations like the Wings, Chariot, Horn, and Neck, which were vital for both timekeeping and state astrology.</summary>

<keywords>Supreme Palace Enclosure, Twenty-Eight Mansions, Vermilion Bird, Azure Dragon, Taiwei Yuan, Yi Mansion, Zhen Mansion, Jiao Mansion, Kang Mansion, Chinese Star Chart</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
The Wing mansion, spanning nineteen degrees, constitutes the <term>Vermilion Bird<note>The Vermilion Bird (Zhuniao) is the celestial symbol of the South, one of the four directional creatures in Chinese astronomy.</note></term> <gloss>wing</gloss>. Master Gan <note>Gan De, a pioneering astronomer of the 4th century BCE.</note> says that it governs the <term>Supreme Tenuity Enclosure<note>Taiwei, one of the three great celestial "enclosures," representing the celestial court and administrative government.</note></term>, the <term>Three Ducal Ministers<note>The Sangong, the highest-ranking civil officials advising the Emperor.</note></term>, the path of moral transformation, and literary records. If the constellation loses its color, the people will wander as refugees. If solar and lunar eclipses occur here, or if the five planets move in retrograde, it is a sign of chaos. If the stars flicker or move restlessly, the path of moral transformation will fail and literary records will be destroyed. If they shift their position, the Three Ducal Ministers will be dismissed. Conversely, if they appear bright and large, the process of civilization will be successfully achieved.

The *Treatise of the Sui Dynasty* <note>The *Sui Zhi*, specifically the astronomical records in the *Book of Sui*.</note> states that the Wing mansion serves as the Emperor's Music Bureau. It presides over entertainers, musicians, and theatrical performances. Furthermore, it governs the <term>foreign tribes<note>Yidi, a general term for non-Han peoples living outside the central Chinese cultural sphere.</note></term>, distant guests, and travelers from across the seas. When the stars are bright and large, rituals are clearly observed, music flourishes, and the tribes from the four directions arrive as honored guests. If the stars move, envoys from the southern tribes will arrive. If they drift from their places, the Emperor will raise an army to campaign against the four directions. 

Some say that brightness indicates the flourishing of ritual and music, while darkness indicates the failure of governance and education. A solar eclipse here suggests ministers overstepping their authority; a lunar eclipse suggests sorrow for women. If the five planets, comets, or "guest stars" invade this mansion, it signifies great misfortune.

Additionally, the five stars of <term>Dong'ou<note>A region corresponding to modern-day eastern Zhejiang province.</note></term> lie to the south of the Wing; these are the stars of the southern tribes. Zhang Heng <note>A famous polymath and astronomer of the Han Dynasty (78–139 CE).</note> says they govern various foreign peoples such as the Dongyue, the <term>Pierced-Chests<note>Chuanxiong, a mythical or distant tribe described in ancient texts like the *Classic of Mountains and Seas*.</note></term>, and the Yueshang. If Venus or Mars <note>Original: "Metal and Fire." In the five elements system, Metal represents Venus and Fire represents Mars.</note> lingers among them, those lands will face war. If these stars flicker, develop pointed rays, or shift, the foreign troops will rebel from within.

<summary>This page describes the astrological significance of the Wing (Yi) mansion, linking its appearance to the stability of the imperial court, the success of cultural education, and the behavior of foreign tribes. It details how celestial movements like eclipses or planetary interventions serve as omens for political dismissal, war, or social unrest.</summary>

<keywords>Wing Mansion, Yi Xiu, Vermilion Bird, Supreme Tenuity, Three Ducal Ministers, Music Bureau, foreign tribes, Zhang Heng, astrological omens, planetary retrograde</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the astronomical section of the Sancai Tuhui (Collected Illustrations of the Three Realms), focusing on the lunar mansions. This section details the Zhen (Chariot) Mansion, its astrological significance, and the secondary stars surrounding it.</meta>

# Collected Illustrations of the Three Realms, Volume 2: Astronomy, Page 13

**The Chariot Mansion** <note>original: 軫, *Zhen*</note> spans seventeen degrees. Master Gan <note>original: 甘氏; referring to Gan De, a famous 4th-century BCE astronomer</note> says that the stars of the Chariot govern generals, the <term>Music Bureau</term> <note>The *Yuefu*, an imperial office responsible for ceremonial music and folk songs</note>, and matters of power and authority. If the five planets trespass against it, officials will lose their positions and ill fortune will strike. Consorts may seize political power, people will lose their livelihoods, and bandit gangs will pillage; disaster will arise within a hundred days.

However, if these stars appear bright and large, the world will prosper, the people will be healthy, and all lands within the <term>Four Seas</term> <note>A traditional term for the boundaries of the known world surrounding China</note> will submit to the King. 

Zhang Heng <note>A renowned 2nd-century CE astronomer and polymath</note> says the Chariot represents the Prime Minister and assisting officials; it governs chariots and cavalry. If it is bright and large, the cavalry is ready for use; another source says if it is bright and large, the cavalry is on the move. 

The *Treatise of the Sui Dynasty* <note>original: 隋志; refers to the astronomical records in the official history of the Sui dynasty</note> states that it governs the transport of loads and also governs the wind. All military movements, whether entering or departing, are divined through the Chariot. Furthermore, it governs death and funerals. If it is bright, chariots are prepared for funeral rites. If the stars flicker, the cavalry is being deployed. If the stars drift or shift from their positions, the Emperor will have cause for worry. If they cluster together, a great army will rise.

The **Linchpin Stars** <note>original: 轄星, *Xia*</note> are attached to both sides of the Chariot. They govern kings and marquises. The **Left Linchpin** represents those of the same surname as the King, while the **Right Linchpin** represents those of different surnames. In divination, it is preferred that they are not too bright; if they are as bright as the four main stars of the Chariot, a great army will rise. If they move far from the Chariot, the country faces great disaster. If the Linchpins appear "raised" or high, it signifies an invasion by the <term>Southern Barbarians</term> <note>original: 南蠻, *Nanman*; a historical term for non-Han peoples to the south</note>. Zhang Heng says that if the Linchpin stars are not visible, the country will face great sorrow. 

The single star **Changsha** <note>named after the region in China</note> sits in the middle of the Chariot; it governs the lifespan of individuals.

<summary>This page describes the Zhen (Chariot) lunar mansion and its associated stars, interpreting their brightness and movements as omens for military action, government stability, and the health of the populace.</summary>
<keywords>Zhen Mansion, Chariot constellation, Gan De, Zhang Heng, astrology, military omens, Linchpin stars, Changsha star, Sancai Tuhui</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the astrological descriptions of the stars associated with the Zhen (Chariot) Mansion, following the previous discussion of its primary stars and their influence on military and civil affairs.</meta>

Also, if the star **Changsha** <note>original: 長沙; likely Gamma Corvi, named after a region in southern China</note> appears bright, the ruler will enjoy a long life and his descendants will flourish. **The Gate of the Army** <note>original: 軍門, *Junmen*</note>, consisting of two stars located to the west of Green Hills, serves as the entrance for the sixteen armies of Heaven; these stars govern military encampments and sentries. 

Regarding the **Leopard's Tail** <note>original: 豹尾, *Baowei*</note> and the **Banner of Awe** <note>original: 威旗, *Weiqi*</note>, if their divination indicates shifting or movement, the roads will become impassable. **The Master of Land** <note>original: 土司空, *Tusikong*</note>, comprised of four yellow stars south of the Gate of the Army, governs earthworks and construction projects. 

Master Wu Xian <note>A legendary shaman-astronomer of the Shang Dynasty</note> says that if Venus <note>original: 金, *Jin*, the "Metal Planet"</note> or Mars <note>original: 火, *Huo*, the "Fire Planet"</note> trespasses against these stars, the fields of the world cannot be plowed and the women will be unable to weave. 

The *Treatise of the Sui Dynasty* <note>original: 隋志; referring to the astronomical records in the official History of Sui</note> states that another name for this is the <term>Minister of Education</term> <note>original: 司徒, *Situ*; an ancient high-ranking official responsible for land and the populace</note>, who governs territorial boundaries. If these stars are bright, it is an auspicious sign.

**Green Hills** <note>original: 青丘, *Qingqiu*</note> consists of seven black stars to the southeast of the Chariot Mansion; they govern the three Han kingdoms of the East <note>Refers to the ancient Korean confederacies of Mahan, Jinhan, and Byeonhan</note>. The divination for these is identical to that of the Eastern stars. 

Thirty-two stars south of the Chariot are called the **Repository of Instruments** <note>original: 器府, *Qifu*</note>, which governs the storehouse of musical instruments. If they appear bright, the musical tones and rhythms are in proper harmony; if they are dim, licentious music will be introduced and the formal ritual music will lose its concord.

<note>original: 春分日在昂卯初昏; this describes the astronomical alignment where the sun resides in the Ang (Pleiades) mansion, while the "Star-Bird" (the constellations of the southern sky) reaches its highest point in the sky—the meridian—at nightfall.</note>

# Diagram of the Culminating Stars of Mid-Summer from the Canon of Yao
<note>The *Canon of Yao* (Yao Dian) is a foundational text of ancient Chinese history. It describes the Emperor Yao's efforts to synchronize the human calendar with the movements of the heavens, specifically identifying "culminating stars"—those that reach the meridian (the highest point in the sky) at dusk during each season.</note>

->**Summer**<-
<note>original: 夏, *Xia*; this character sits at the very center of the diagram, identifying the season being mapped.</note>

<margin>
**Mid-Summer**
<note>original: 仲夏, *Zhongxia*; the second month of the summer season, which includes the Summer Solstice.</note>
</margin>

<margin>
At the **Summer Solstice**, the sun is in the **Star Mansion**; at dusk, the [Fire] Star is at the meridian.
<note>original: 夏至日在星初昏; this explains that on the longest day of the year, the sun is positioned in the lunar mansion known as "The Star" (Xing), while the primary seasonal star reaches the center of the night sky at nightfall.</note>
</margin>

<margin>
The **Great Fire** is at the center of the seven mansions.
<note>original: 大火正七宿之中, *Dahuo zheng qixiu zhi zhong*. "Great Fire" is the ancient name for the red star Antares. It serves as the central marker for the seven lunar mansions of the Azure Dragon of the East.</note>
</margin>

<margin>
**The Star and the Fire**
<note>original: 星火, *Xinghuo*; these are the two vital celestial markers for this season mentioned in the classic text.</note>
</margin>

***

### The Twelve Earthly Branches
<note>The square borders of the diagram contain the Twelve Earthly Branches, which correspond to both directions and months of the year. They are arranged here to show the orientation of the cosmos.</note>

->The Serpent (Si), The Horse (Wu), The Goat (Wei)<-
<note>The southern direction, associated with the heat of summer.</note>

->The Monkey (Shen), The Rooster (You), The Dog (Xu)<-
<note>The western direction, associated with the harvest of autumn.</note>

->The Pig (Hai), The Rat (Zi), The Ox (Chou)<-
<note>The northern direction, associated with the cold of winter.</note>

->The Tiger (Yin), The Rabbit (Mao), The Dragon (Chen)<-
<note>The eastern direction, associated with the growth of spring.</note>

***

<note>The concentric rings within the diagram list the names of the **Twenty-Eight Mansions** (lunar zodiac), showing how they rotate around the central axis of the seasons. By observing which of these mansions appeared directly overhead at dusk, ancient astronomers could tell exactly when to inform farmers that mid-summer had arrived.</note>

**Technical Vocabulary:**
- <term>The Star Mansion (Xing)</term>: One of the seven mansions of the Vermilion Bird of the South; it consists of seven stars, with Alphard being the most prominent.
- <term>The Great Fire (Dahuo)</term>: A significant astronomical marker in ancient China, specifically referring to the star Antares in the Heart Mansion. Its appearance was used to regulate the agricultural calendar.
- <term>Meridian (Zhong)</term>: The imaginary line running from north to south through the zenith; when a star "culminates" or reaches this point, it is at its highest point in the sky.

# Diagram of the Culminating Stars of Mid-Autumn from the Canon of Yao
<note>The *Canon of Yao* (Yao Dian) continues its seasonal cycle here. This diagram illustrates how the ancient Chinese observers determined the arrival of Mid-Autumn by observing which star reached the meridian (the highest point in the sky) at dusk during the Autumnal Equinox.</note>

->**Autumn**<-
<note>original: 秋, *Qiu*; this character marks the center of the diagram, indicating the season of the harvest.</note>

<margin>
**Mid-Autumn**
<note>original: 仲秋, *Zhongqiu*; the second month of autumn, containing the Autumnal Equinox.</note>
</margin>

<margin>
At the **Autumnal Equinox**, the sun is in the **Room Mansion** at dusk.
<note>original: 秋分日在房初昏; *Qiufen ri zai Fang chu hun*. This technical note explains that when the sun sets on the day of the equinox, its position against the background of the stars (calculated by its opposite) is in the "Room" (Fang) lunar mansion.</note>
</margin>

<margin>
The star **Emptiness** is at the center of the seven mansions.
<note>original: 星虛正七宿之中, *Xing Xu zheng qixiu zhi zhong*. The "Emptiness" (Xu) mansion is the central marker of the seven mansions belonging to the Black Tortoise of the North.</note>
</margin>

<margin>
**Equal Night and the star Emptiness**
<note>original: 宵中星虛, *Xiaozhong xing Xu*. "Xiao zhong" literally means "the middle of the night," referring to the equinox where the night and day are of equal length (12 hours each). At this moment, the "Emptiness" star culminates at dusk.</note>
</margin>

***

### The Celestial Coordinates

The diagram is structured with a square outer border representing the Earthly directions and a circular inner heaven.

**The Twelve Earthly Branches (Outer Square)**
<note>These branches serve as a cosmic compass and a way to track the months and hours.</note>

| Direction | Earthly Branches |
| :--- | :--- |
| **South (Top)** | Snake (巳), Horse (午), Goat (未) |
| **West (Right)** | Monkey (申), Rooster (酉), Dog (戌) |
| **North (Bottom)** | Pig (亥), Rat (子), Ox (丑) |
| **East (Left)** | Tiger (寅), Rabbit (卯), Dragon (辰) |

**The Twenty-Eight Lunar Mansions (Inner Rings)**
<note>These are the 28 "stations" or constellations the moon passes through during its monthly cycle. In this Mid-Autumn diagram, the focus is on the Northern and Eastern sectors.</note>

*   **The Culminating Mansion:** **Emptiness** (虛, *Xu*) <note>This star reaches the center of the sky at dusk to signal the peak of Autumn.</note>
*   **Surrounding Mansions include:** 
    *   Rooftop (危), Encampment (室), Wall (壁) — *The Northern sector*
    *   Stride (奎), Bond (婁), Stomach (胃), Hairy Head (昴), Net (畢), Turtle Beak (觜), Three Stars (參) — *The Western sector*
    *   Well (井), Ghost (鬼), Willow (柳), Star (星), Extended Net (張), Wings (翼), Chariot (軫) — *The Southern sector*
    *   Horn (角), Neck (亢), Root (氐), Room (房), Heart (心), Tail (尾), Winnowing Basket (箕) — *The Eastern sector*
    *   Dipper (斗), Ox (牛), Girl (女) — *The Northern sector*

It completes the third part of a four-season celestial cycle used by ancient rulers to regulate the agricultural calendar.</summary>

->## Collected Illustrations of the Three Realms, Volume 3: Astronomy<-

# Diagram of the Culminating Stars of Mid-Winter from the Canon of Yao
<note>The *Canon of Yao* (Yao Dian) is one of the oldest documents in the Chinese tradition, detailing how the legendary Emperor Yao commanded astronomers to observe the stars to create a calendar. This diagram shows the specific constellations visible during the shortest days of the year.</note>

->**Winter**<-
<note>original: 冬, *Dong*; this central character identifies the season. In the five-element system, winter is associated with water, the north, and the color black.</note>

<margin>
**Collected Illustrations of the Three Realms, Volume 3**
**Astronomy**
</margin>

<margin>
**Mid-Winter**
<note>original: 仲冬, *Zhongdong*; refers to the eleventh lunar month, the height of the winter season.</note>
</margin>

<margin>
At the **Winter Solstice**, the sun is in the **Emptiness Mansion** at dusk.
<note>original: 冬至日在虛初昏; *Dongzhi ri zai Xu chu hun*. The "Emptiness" (Xu) mansion is one of the constellations in the northern sky. This technical note tells the reader that on the solstice, the sun's position—if it could be seen against the stars—would be in this mansion at nightfall.</note>
</margin>

<margin>
The star **Pleiades** is at the center of the seven mansions.
<note>original: 星昴正七宿之中; *Xing Mao zheng qixiu zhi zhong*. "Mao" refers to the Pleiades star cluster. In the *Canon of Yao*, the Pleiades is the "meridian star" (zhong xing) for winter, meaning it reaches its highest point in the southern sky at dusk during this season.</note>
</margin>

***

### The Celestial Coordinates
<note>The diagram depicts the "Twenty-Eight Mansions" (lunar constellations) arranged in a circle, nested within a square representing the earthly directions.</note>

**The Twelve Earthly Branches (Outer Frame):**
The square frame marks the cardinal directions and the hours of the day. Note that in traditional Chinese maps, **South** is at the top.
*   **South (Noon):** Horse (午) at the top.
*   **North (Midnight):** Rat (子) at the bottom.
*   **East (Dawn):** Rabbit (卯) at the left.
*   **West (Dusk):** Rooster (酉) at the right.

**The Twenty-Eight Mansions (The Circle):**
The circle is divided into the four "Images" or seasonal quadrants. The segments include:
*   **The Northern Black Tortoise:** Including **Emptiness** (虛), **Rooftop** (危), and **Room** (室).
*   **The Western White Tiger:** Including **Pleiades** (昴), **Net** (畢), and **Turtle Beak** (觜).
*   **The Southern Vermilion Bird:** Including **Willow** (柳), **Star** (星), and **Extended Net** (張).
*   **The Eastern Azure Dragon:** Including **Horn** (角), **Neck** (亢), and **Heart** (心).

**Solar Terms and Stations:**
The inner segments record the "Twelve Stations" (the Chinese zodiac), such as **Great Fire** (大火), **Great Beam** (大梁), **Quail's Head** (鶉首), and **Quail's Fire** (鶉火). These were used to track the movement of the planet Jupiter and the Sun.

<summary>This diagram illustrates the astronomical observations for Mid-Winter as recorded in the Canon of Yao, specifically identifying the Pleiades as the star that culminates at dusk during the Winter Solstice. It demonstrates the sophisticated ancient Chinese system of mapping the "Twenty-Eight Mansions" against the "Twelve Earthly Branches."</summary>

<keywords>Canon of Yao, Mid-Winter, Winter Solstice, Pleiades, Emptiness Mansion, Twenty-Eight Mansions, Twelve Earthly Branches, Sancai Tuhui, Ancient Chinese Astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of celestial mechanics, specifically focusing on the positioning of the lunar mansions relative to the seasons and the rotation of the heavens.</meta>

The <term>Twenty-Eight Mansions</term> <note>original: 二十八宿; these are the 28 constellations along the ecliptic through which the moon passes</note> are arranged in a ring across the four quarters of the sky, rotating westward along with the heavens. The seven mansions of the East, from the Horn mansion to the Winnowing Basket mansion, constitute the <term>Azure Dragon</term>. In terms of their sequential order, the Room and Heart mansions represent the center of the <term>Great Fire</term> <note>original: 大火; the ancient name for the star Antares, used as a seasonal marker</note>. The seven mansions of the South, from the Well mansion to the Chariot mansion, constitute the <term>Quail Bird</term>; if spoken of in terms of its shape, it has the image of the <term>Vermilion Bird</term>. <term>Emptiness</term> <note>original: 虛, Xu</note> is the central star among the seven mansions of the North. <term>Hairy Head</term> <note>original: 昴, Mao; known in the West as the Pleiades</note> is the central star among the seven mansions of the West.

The stars themselves do not actually move; they move only because they are attached to the heavens <note>Ancient Chinese astronomers believed the stars were fixed to a physical celestial sphere that rotated around the Earth</note>. The heavens tilt toward the northwest, while the Pole remains in the center of the sky. The Twenty-Eight Mansions are half hidden and half visible <note>relative to the horizon</note>, each appearing according to its proper season. This is why one must observe and examine them from the south.

In the <term>Middle Month of Spring</term> <note>original: 仲春, Zhongchun; the second month of the traditional lunar calendar</note>, the Star of Fire is in the east, the Star of the Bird is in the south, the Star of the Hairy Head is in the west, and the Star of Emptiness is in the north.

When it reaches <term>Mid-Summer</term>, the Bird has turned to the west, Fire has turned to the south, Emptiness has turned to the east, and the Hairy Head has turned to the north.

In <term>Mid-Autumn</term>, Fire has turned to the west, Emptiness has turned to the south, the Hairy Head has turned to the east, and the Bird has turned to the north.

By the time of <term>Mid-Winter</term>, Emptiness has turned to the west, the Hairy Head has turned to the south, the Bird has turned to the east, and Fire has turned to the north. In the coming year...

<summary>This page describes the configuration of the Twenty-Eight Mansions (the Chinese zodiac) and explains how the central stars of the four celestial quadrants rotate through the cardinal directions across the four seasons.</summary>
<keywords>Twenty-Eight Mansions, Azure Dragon, Vermilion Bird, Antares (Great Fire), Pleiades (Mao), seasonal astronomy, celestial rotation</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the astronomical methods used in antiquity to determine the seasons. It specifically contrasts the "Canon of Yao" method—focusing on the four cardinal seasonal markers—with the "Monthly Ordinances" (Yueling) system, which provides a more granular month-by-month celestial guide.</meta>

In the <term>Middle Month of Spring</term> <note>original: 仲春, Zhongchun; the second month of the traditional lunar calendar, containing the Spring Equinox</note>, the <term>Bird star</term> <note>original: 鳥, Niao; the central constellation of the southern sky, associated with the Vermilion Bird</note> aligns its position and revolves toward the south in an endless cycle. This follows the <term>Canon of Yao</term> <note>original: 堯典, Yaodian; a foundational chapter of the *Classic of History* detailing the astronomical mandates of the legendary Emperor Yao</note>, which examines the <term>meridian stars</term> <note>original: 中星, Zhongxing; stars that cross the local meridian at dusk, used to determine the exact time of year</note> to rectify the timing of the <term>four seasons</term>. This system is simple and clear, differing from the <term>Monthly Ordinances</term> <note>original: 月令, Yueling; a ritual calendar found in the *Book of Rites*</note>, where the stars are cited based on the specific root or beginning of each individual month.

---

# Star Chart of the Mid-Spring Heavens

This diagram illustrates the celestial bureaucracy as seen in the night sky. In the Chinese tradition, stars were not just points of light but reflected the imperial court on Earth, featuring officials, markets, kitchens, and even domestic animals.

->**The Celestial Realm**<-

# Star Chart of the Second Lunar Month

This diagram acts as a celestial calendar, mapping the positions of the stars to the passing of time on Earth. In this system, the sky is divided into twenty-eight "mansions" or lodges that the moon and sun pass through. By observing which stars are directly overhead at dusk and dawn, ancient astronomers could precisely determine the time of year.

->**Positions of the Heavens**<-

### The Solar and Stellar Transit
**In the second month, the sun is located in the Legs mansion.** <note>original: 二月日在奎; "Legs" (Kui) is the fifteenth lunar mansion, roughly corresponding to the constellation Andromeda.</note> **At dusk, the Bow asterism is at the meridian.** <note>original: 昏弧中; the "Bow" (Hu) is a group of stars in the modern constellations Canis Major and Puppis. To be "at the meridian" (zhong) means the stars have reached their highest point in the southern sky.</note> **At dawn, the Establishment stars are at the meridian.** <note>original: 旦建星中; "Establishment" (Jianxing) consists of six stars in Sagittarius.</note>

<column-break/>

### Significant Asterisms
The chart identifies several prominent groupings of stars that mirrored the world below:

**The Three Stars**
<note>original: 参, Shen; one of the most famous Chinese mansions, corresponding to the belt and body of Orion. It was often associated with a great general.</note>

**The Well**
<note>original: 井, Jing; representing a celestial well for the palace, located in the constellation Gemini.</note>

**The Celestial Wolf**
<term>Celestial Wolf</term> <note>original: 狼, Lang; known in Western astronomy as Sirius, the brightest star in the sky. It was often seen as a symbol of potential invasion or "thievery" from the borders.</note>

**The Wild Pheasant**
<note>original: 野雞, Yejie; a single star located near the Wolf.</note>

**Interpreters of the Nine Provinces**
<note>original: 九州殊口, Jiuzhou Zhukou; a group of stars representing the translators who facilitated communication between the various regions of the empire.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the star chart for the second lunar month, noting that the sun resides in the Legs mansion while the Bow and Establishment asterisms mark the meridian at dusk and dawn respectively. It highlights key constellations including the Celestial Wolf (Sirius) and the Three Stars (Orion).</summary>

<keywords>Chinese astronomy, Second Lunar Month, Legs Mansion, Celestial Wolf, Sirius, Orion's Belt, star chart, meridian transit</keywords>

# Star Chart of the Second Lunar Month

This diagram acts as a celestial calendar, mapping the positions of the stars to the passing of time on Earth. In this system, the sky is divided into twenty-eight "mansions" or lodges that the moon and sun pass through. By observing which stars are directly overhead at dusk and dawn, ancient astronomers could precisely determine the time of year.

->**Positions of the Heavens**<-

### The Solar and Stellar Transit
**In the second month, the sun is located in the Legs mansion.** <note>original: 二月日在奎; "Legs" (Kui) is the fifteenth lunar mansion, roughly corresponding to the constellation Andromeda.</note> **At dusk, the Bow asterism is at the meridian.** <note>original: 昏弧中; the "Bow" (Hushi/Hu) is a group of stars in the modern constellations Canis Major and Puppis. To be "at the meridian" (zhong) means the stars have reached their highest point in the southern sky.</note> **At dawn, the Establishment stars are at the meridian.** <note>original: 旦建星中; "Establishment" (Jianxing) consists of six stars in Sagittarius.</note>

<column-break/>

The chart identifies the three primary regions of the sky, organized like a celestial empire:

**The Supreme Palace Enclosure**
<note>original: 太微垣, Taiwei Yuan; the administrative center of the heavens, where high officials and court ministers reside.</note>

**The Celestial Market Enclosure**
<note>original: 天市垣, Tianshi Yuan; the outer region representing the public marketplace and the various regional lords.</note>
