# Celestial Map of the Second Lunar Month

This map serves as a visual guide to the heavens as they appear during the second month of the lunar year. By tracking the "meridian" stars—those that reach the highest point in the sky at dusk and dawn—ancient observers could calibrate the calendar and predict the arrival of spring's peak.

->**Observations of the Solar Path**<-

**In the second month, the sun is located in the Stomach mansion.** <note>original: 二月日在胃; the "Stomach" (Wei) mansion corresponds to stars in the modern constellation Aries. This indicates the sun's position along the ecliptic during this time of year.</note> **At dusk, the Seven Stars are at the meridian.** <note>original: 昏七星中; the "Seven Stars" (Qixing) is a mansion in the constellation Hydra. When it is "at the meridian," it is positioned directly south of the observer at sunset.</note> **At dawn, the Ox-Leader is at the meridian.** <note>original: 旦牽牛中; the "Ox-Leader" (Qianniu), often simply called the "Ox" (Niu), corresponds to part of Capricornus.</note>

<column-break/>

<note>original: 紫微垣; the central celestial region representing the Emperor's palace.</note>

<note>original: 柱, Zhu; stars supporting the celestial canopy.</note>

# Celestial Map of the Fourth Lunar Month

->In the fourth month, the sun is in the Net mansion. At dusk, the Wings mansion is central; at dawn, the Maiden mansion is central.<-
<note>original: 四月日在畢昏翼中旦婺女中. The "Net" (Bi) is a mansion in Taurus. The "Wings" (Yi) is in Crater/Corvus, and the "Maiden" (Wunü) is in Aquarius. "Central" refers to the mansion crossing the observer's meridian.</note>

### The Northern and Circumpolar Regions
**The Purple Forbidden Enclosure** <note>original: 紫微垣, Ziweiyuan; the innermost celestial enclosure surrounding the North Pole, representing the private palace of the Emperor and his family.</note>
**The North Pole** <note>original: 北極, Beiji; the pivot of the rotating heavens.</note>
**The Curved Array** <note>original: 勾陳, Gouchen; an asterism including the modern North Star, representing the Emperor's consort or the rear palace.</note>
**The Celestial Pivot** <note>original: 天樞, Tianshu; the first star of the Northern Dipper (Dubhe in Ursa Major).</note>
**The Canopy** and **The Canopy Support** <note>original: 華蓋 (Huagai) and 杠 (Gang); a group of stars in Cassiopeia representing the imperial umbrella.</note>
**The Guest House** <note>original: 傳舍, Chuanshe; a celestial inn for foreign envoys or traveling officials.</note>

### The Administrative Palace and Guards

**The General of the Guards** <note>original: 郎將, Langjiang; the high-ranking military officer responsible for palace security.</note>
**The Favored Official** <note>original: 幸臣, Xingchen; a star representing a courtier who has the particular ear of the Emperor.</note>
**The Inner Steps** <note>original: 內階, Neijie; steps leading up to the imperial dais.</note>

### The Western and Southern Auxiliaries

# Celestial Map of the Market and the Mansions

### The Pillars and Attendants of the High Heavens
**The Right Pivot** and **The Left Pivot** <note>original: 右樞 (Youshu) and 左樞 (Zuoshu); these stars act as guardians at the entrance to the Purple Forbidden Enclosure.</note>
**The Celestial Spear** <note>original: 天槍, Tianqiang; a three-star asterism located in Boötes.</note>
**The Dark Lance** <note>original: 玄戈, Xuange; a star representing a dark-colored weapon, often associated with the defense of the celestial palace.</note>
**The Beckoning Shaking** <note>original: 招搖, Zhaoyao; the star Gamma Boötis, traditionally seen as a pointing finger or signal.</note>

### The Celestial Market Enclosure
->The Celestial Market represents the great trading centers of the empire, where the wealth of the world is gathered and regulated.<-
<note>original: 天市垣, Tianshiyuan; the third of the "Three Enclosures," representing the public marketplace and administrative courts.</note>

**The Emperor** <note>original: 帝, Di; the central authority of the market (Alpha Herculis).</note>
**The Attendant** <note>original: 候, Hou; an official who observes the market's activities.</note>
**The Patriarchal Rectifier** and **The Patriarchal Men** <note>original: 宗正 (Zongzheng) and 宗人 (Zongren); officials responsible for the imperial clan and lineage within the court structure.</note>
**The Patriarch** <note>original: 宗, Zong; the ancestral head.</note>
**The Cloth Measure** <note>original: 帛度, Bodu; a star used to represent the measurement of textiles.</note>
**The Butcher’s Shop** <note>original: 屠肆, Tusi; the celestial representation of the meat market.</note>
**The Row of Shops**, **The Chariot Shop**, and **The Market Tower** <note>original: 列肆 (Liesi), 車肆 (Chesi), and 市樓 (Shilou); these represent the various commercial stalls and the central watchtower governing trade.</note>

**The Coiled Thong** <note>original: 貫索, Guansuo; the "Jeweled Necklace" or Corona Borealis, representing a prison or a circle of merchants.</note>
**The Seven Lords** <note>original: 七公, Qigong; seven high officials acting as advisors.</note>
**The Celestial Record** <note>original: 天紀, Tianji; the record-keepers who maintain order and discipline.</note>
**The Maidservant’s Bed** <note>original: 女床, Nvchuang; the quarters for palace maids.</note>
**The Celestial Flail** and **The Support Basket** <note>original: 天棓 (Tianbang) and 扶筐 (Fukuang); agricultural tools used for threshing grain and gathering mulberry leaves.</note>
**The Celestial Pivot** <note>original: 天崗, Tiangang; a guiding star in the northern sky.</note>

<column-break/>

### The High Officials and Ministers
**The Great Horn** <note>original: 大角, Dajue; the brilliant star Arcturus, seen as the "Golden Throne" or a celestial palace guard.</note>
**The Attendant Steps** <note>original: 攝提, Sheti; stars that assist the Great Horn in marking the seasons.</note>
**The Nine Ministers** and **The Three Excellencies** <note>original: 九卿 (Jiuqing) and 三公 (Sangong); the highest-ranking bureaucratic officials in the imperial administration.</note>
**The Minister** <note>original: 相, Xiang; the Prime Minister or chancellor.</note>
**The Celestial Milk** <note>original: 天乳, Tianru; representing nourishment or the wet nurse of the imperial family.</note>

### The Twenty-Eight Lunar Mansions
<note>These are the stations the moon passes through during its monthly cycle, divided into the four directions (The Azure Dragon, the Black Tortoise, the White Tiger, and the Vermilion Bird).</note>

*   **The Root** (Di), **The Neck** (Kang), **The Horn** (Jiao)
*   **The Chariot** (Zhen), **The Wings** (Yi), **The Extended Net** (Zhang)
*   **The Star** (Xing), **The Willow** (Liu), **The Ghost** (Gui)
*   **The Well** (Jing), **The Three Stars** (Shen), **The Turtle Beak** (Zi)
*   **The Net** (Bi), **The Hairy Head** (Mao), **The Stomach** (Wei)
*   **The Bond** (Lou), **The Legs** (Kui), **The Wall** (Bi)
*   **The Encampment** (Shi), **The Rooftop** (Wei), **The Emptiness** (Xu)
*   **The Maiden** (Nü), **The Ox** (Niu), **The Dipper** (Dou)
*   **The Winnowing Basket** (Ji), **The Tail** (Wei), **The Heart** (Xin), **The Room** (Fang)

### Southern Stars and Auspicious Markers
**The Arsenal** <note>original: 庫樓, Kulou; a military storehouse located in the constellation Centaurus.</note>
**The Southern Gate** <note>original: 南門, Nanmen; Alpha and Beta Centauri, marking the southernmost entrance of the sky.</note>
**The Level Path** <note>original: 平道, Pingdao; a road for the Emperor's chariot.</note>
**The Advancing Worthy** <note>original: 進賢, Jinxian; the star for nominating virtuous officials.</note>
**The Tripods of Zhou** <note>original: 周鼎, Zhouding; symbols of state power and ritual legitimacy.</note>
**The Celestial Cup**, **The Celestial River**, and **The Celestial Key** <note>original: 天尊 (Tianzun), 天江 (Tianjiang), and 天籥 (Tianyue); symbols of abundance, the flow of the Milky Way, and the unlocking of celestial mysteries.</note>

### ### Star Chart

<note>The following list translates the labels found across the woodblock star chart. In traditional Chinese uranography, stars are often named after terrestrial officials, architectural features, or everyday objects, reflecting the belief that the heavens mirror the earthly imperial state.</note>

**Right Pivot** <note>original: 右樞, Youshu; a key star in the Dragon constellation (Draco) acting as a boundary for the inner palace.</note>
**Secretary of State** <note>original: 尚書, Shangshu</note>
**Crown Prince** <note>original: 太子, Taizi</note>
**The Emperor** <note>original: 帝, Di</note>
**Son of a Concubine** <note>original: 庶子, Shuzi</note>
**Empress and Consorts** <note>original: 后妃, Houfei</note>
**Celestial Bed** <note>original: 天床, Tianchuang</note>
**Grand Judge** <note>original: 大理, Dali</note>
**Left Pivot** <note>original: 左樞, Zuoshu</note>
**Celestial Spear** <note>original: 天槍, Tianqiang</note>
**Dark Lance** <note>original: 玄戈, Xuange</note>
**Beckoning Shaker** <note>original: 招搖, Zhaoyao; Boötes constellation.</note>
**Celestial River-Bridge** <note>original: 梗河, Genghe</note>
**Great Horn** <note>original: 大角, Dajiao; the star Arcturus.</note>

**Key Terms Found in this Map:**
*   **Star Chart** <term>星圖 (Xingtu): A visual representation of the heavens used for divination and timekeeping.</term>
*   **Heavenly Market Enclosure** <term>天市垣 (Tianshiyuan): One of the Three Enclosures, representing the marketplace and provincial governments.</term>
*   **Purple Forbidden Enclosure** <term>紫微垣 (Ziweiyuan): The central enclosure surrounding the North Pole, representing the Emperor's private palace.</term>
*   **The Conductor** <term>攝提 (Sheti): Stars that "conduct" or point toward the Great Horn (Arcturus).</term>

It illustrates how the ancient Chinese mapped their social and political structures—from the Emperor and his family to butchers' stalls and liquid measures—onto the night sky.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the central Purple Forbidden Enclosure described previously toward the Supreme Palace Enclosure and the lunar mansions of the southern and eastern skies. It provides the seasonal positioning for the sixth lunar month, aligning the movement of the sun with the visibility of specific "mansions" (constellations along the ecliptic) and significant stars.</meta>

->**In the sixth month, the Sun is in the Willow Mansion; at dusk, the Fire Star is at the meridian, and at dawn, the Astraddle Mansion is at the meridian.**<-

<note>This text describes the celestial alignment for the height of summer. The "Fire Star" (original: 火, *Huo*) refers to the red supergiant Antares in the Heart Mansion. "At the meridian" (original: 中, *zhong*) means the star is at its highest point in the sky at the specified time.</note>

# In the Seventh Month, the Sun is in the Wing Mansion; at Dusk the Establishment Stars are Central, at Dawn the Net Mansion is Central

<note>original: 七月日在翼昏建星中旦畢中. This title describes the seasonal positions of the stars. The "Wing" (Yi) is a lunar mansion in the constellation Crater; "Establishment" (Jian) is in Sagittarius; and the "Net" (Bi) is in Taurus.</note>

# Star Chart of the Northern and Equatorial Skies

<note>This diagram serves as a celestial map, or *Xingtu*, illustrating the constellations as they were understood in traditional Chinese astronomy. The stars are organized into "Enclosures" and "Mansions," mirroring the terrestrial hierarchy of the imperial court, the marketplace, and the military.</note>

### I. The Circumpolar Region
*The stars surrounding the North Celestial Pole, representing the inner sanctum of the Emperor.*

*   **Lesser Guard** <note>original: 少衛, *Shaowei*</note>
*   **Maidservants** <note>original: 御女, *Yunü*</note>
*   **The Pillars** <note>original: 柱, *Zhu*</note>
*   **Great Honor** <note>original: 大尊, *Dazun*</note>
*   **Royal Secretary** <note>original: 尚書, *Shangshu*</note>
*   **Lesser Steward** <note>original: 少宰, *Shaozai*</note>
*   **Lesser Guard (Military)** <note>original: 少尉, *Shaowei*</note>
*   **Lesser Official** <note>original: 少吏, *Shaoli*</note>
*   **Attendant Servants** <note>original: 侍僕, *Shipu*</note>
*   **Upper Assistant** <note>original: 上輔, *Shangfu*</note>
*   **The Heavenly Kitchen** <note>original: 天廚, *Tianchu*</note>
*   **The Heavenly Hook** <note>original: 天勾, *Tiangou*</note>
*   **Zao Fu** <note>original: 造父; a legendary charioteer of King Mu of Zhou.</note>
*   **The State Umbrella** <note>original: 華蓋, *Huagai*</note>
*   **The Parasol Handle** <note>original: 杠, *Gang*</note>
*   **The Guest House** <note>original: 傳舍, *Chuanshe*</note>

---

### II. The Chariots and Roadways
*Asterisms representing transport, travel, and the crossing of the Milky Way.*

*   **The Carriage Yard** <note>original: 車府, *Chefufu*</note>
*   **The Restricted Palace** <note>original: 離宮, *Ligong*; also known as the Summer Palace.</note>
*   **The Flying Serpent** <note>original: 騰蛇, *Tengshe*</note>
*   **Officer of Earthworks** <note>original: 土公吏, *Tugongli*</note>
*   **Thunder and Lightning** <note>original: 雷電, *Leidian*</note>
*   **The Gallery Road** <note>original: 閣道, *Gedao*</note>
*   **Wang Liang** <note>original: 王良; another famous charioteer of the Spring and Autumn period.</note>
*   **The Whip** <note>original: 策, *Ce*</note>
*   **The Attached Road** <note>original: 附路, *Fulu*</note>

# Star Chart of the Northern and Equatorial Skies

->In the eighth month, the Sun resides in the **Horn** mansion; at dusk, the **Ox** mansion is at the meridian, and at dawn, the **Turtle Beak** mansion is at the meridian.<-

### Summary of Astronomical Symbols
In Chinese uranography, the stars are not merely clusters of light but mirror the earthly social order. The **Purple Forbidden Enclosure** represents the inner court where the Emperor and his family reside. In contrast, the **Heavenly Market Enclosure** represents the economic life of the empire, featuring stalls for butchers and cloth merchants. This specific chart emphasizes the transition into autumn, highlighting the **Lunar Mansions** from the "Neck" to the "Ox," which the moon passes through during this season.

<summary>This page catalogs star groups within the Purple Forbidden and Heavenly Market Enclosures, along with several lunar mansions, defining the celestial geography as a reflection of the Imperial court and public life.</summary>
<keywords>Purple Forbidden Enclosure, Heavenly Market Enclosure, Lunar Mansions, Weaving Maid, Altair, 28 Mansions, Chinese Astronomy</keywords>

# Star Chart of the Northern and Equatorial Skies

->In the ninth month, the Sun resides in the **Room** mansion; at dusk, the **Void** mansion is at the meridian, and at dawn, the **Willow** mansion is at the meridian.<-
<note>original: 九月日在房昏虛中旦柳中. This note tracks the Sun’s progress through the twenty-eight lunar mansions. As the year turns toward late autumn, the stars of the "Void" (in Aquarius) reach their highest point at sunset, while the "Willow" (in Hydra) appears at the horizon as day breaks.</note>

<margin>
**Purple Forbidden Enclosure** <note>original: 紫微宮, *Ziwei Gong*. The central area of the sky representing the Emperor’s private palace, containing stars that never set.</note>

**The Great One** <note>original: 太乙, *Taiyi*. A primordial celestial deity associated with the pole star.</note>

</margin>

<margin>

**Broken Mortar** <note>original: 敗臼, *Baijiu*.</note>

**Imperial Clansmen** <note>original: 宗人, *Zongren*.</note>
**Measure of Cloth** <note>original: 帛度, *Bodu*. Representing the standardization of textiles in the market.</note>
</margin>

# Star Chart of the Northern and Equatorial Skies

->In the tenth month, the Sun resides in the **Tail** mansion; at dusk, the **Rooftop** mansion is at the meridian, and at dawn, the **Seven Stars** mansion is at the meridian.<-
<note>original: 十月日在尾昏危中旦七星中. This marks the transition into early winter. The "Tail" (in Scorpius) marks the sun's position, while the "Rooftop" (in Aquarius/Pegasus) and "Seven Stars" (in Hydra) serve as timekeepers for the beginning and end of the night.</note>

<margin>
**Purple Forbidden Enclosure**
<note>original: 紫微垣, *Ziwei Yuan*. The central zone of the sky representing the celestial palace of the Emperor, where the stars never set.</note>

<note>original: 天市垣, *Tianshi Yuan*. One of the Three Enclosures, representing the celestial marketplace and the various regional states of the empire.</note>
**Emperor's Throne** <note>original: 帝座, *Dizuo*.</note>
**The Watchman** <note>original: 候, *Hou*.</note>

**Graduated Terrace** <note>original: 漸臺, *Jiantai*. Located near the Weaving Girl, associated with water clocks or astronomical instruments.</note>

</margin>

# Star Chart of the Western Quarter and the Celestial River

->This map depicts the stars visible during the transition from autumn to winter, centered around the Celestial River and the lunar mansions of the West.<-

The Celestial River <gloss>original: 天河, *Tianhe*; The Milky Way, seen here as a flowing band of light across the upper sky.</gloss>

Five Chariots <gloss>original: 五車, *Wuche*; A group of five bright stars representing the transport of the five celestial emperors.</gloss>

Feudal Kings <gloss>original: 諸王, *Zhuwang*; Stars representing the regional lords who serve the central emperor.</gloss>

Eight Spirits <gloss>original: 八魁, *Bakui*; Also known as the 'Net for Catching Birds,' these stars relate to hunting and the harvest.</gloss>

Rolled Tongue <gloss>original: 卷舌, *Juanshe*; An asterism in Perseus; its appearance was traditionally used to divine the prevalence of gossip or slander in the empire.</gloss>

Celestial Slander <gloss>original: 天讒, *Tianchan*; A lone star associated with the Rolled Tongue, symbolizing malicious speech.</gloss>

Whetstone <gloss>original: 礪石, *Lishi*; Represents a stone for sharpening tools and weapons.</gloss>

**The Net** <gloss>original: 畢, *Bi*; The 19th Lunar Mansion. Shaped like a hunting net, it is also associated with the arrival of rain.</gloss>

Celestial Gate <gloss>original: 天關, *Tianguan*; Located on the ecliptic, this "gate" marks the path where the sun, moon, and planets pass.</gloss>

**The Hairy Head** <gloss>original: 昴, *Mao*; The 18th Lunar Mansion, known in the West as the Pleiades cluster. Its name refers to the "mane" or "hair" of the White Tiger.</gloss>

Celestial River Bank <gloss>original: 天阿, *Tian'a*; The embankment or shoreline of the Milky Way.</gloss>

The Moon <gloss>original: 月, *Yue*; Depicted here to show its path through the lunar mansions.</gloss>

**The Stomach** <gloss>original: 胃, *Wei*; The 17th Lunar Mansion, representing the celestial granary or the stomach of the heavens, symbolizing food supply and wealth.</gloss>

Celestial Granary <gloss>original: 天廩, *Tianlin*; A storehouse for the sacrificial grain used in imperial rites.</gloss>

Celestial Meadows <gloss>original: 天苑, *Tianyuan*; The imperial hunting park or meadows where animals are kept.</gloss>

Circular Celestial Granary <gloss>original: 天囷, *Tianqun*; A round storage bin for grain, emphasizing the celestial focus on agricultural abundance.</gloss>

Nine Flags <gloss>original: 九斿, *Jiuliu*; Representing the banners of the emperor, these stars are found near the border of the modern constellations Orion and Eridanus.</gloss>

Banner of Three Stars <gloss>original: 參旗, *Canqi*; A celestial flag located near the great hunter Orion.</gloss>

Jade Well <gloss>original: 玉井, *Yujing*; A well located near the foot of the Hunter (Orion) providing water for the celestial court.</gloss>

Screen <gloss>original: 屏, *Ping*; A folding screen used to provide privacy for the celestial toilet.</gloss>

Military Well <gloss>original: 軍井, *Junjing*; A water source specifically for the use of the celestial armies.</gloss>

Celestial Toilet <gloss>original: 天廁, *Tiance*; A group of four stars below Orion, representing the necessary but humble aspects of the celestial palace.</gloss>

Celestial Arrow <gloss>original: 天矢, *Tianshi*; Located near the toilet. <note>While literally "Arrow," in some astronomical traditions, this star is also associated with the "Celestial Excrement" (天屎) due to its proximity to the Toilet.</note></gloss>

<summary>This page presents a detailed woodblock star chart identifying key lunar mansions and constellations in the western sky. It illustrates the Chinese tradition of projecting an entire earthly imperial bureaucracy and its agricultural needs—granaries, wells, and even toilets—onto the stars.</summary>

<keywords>Star Chart, Lunar Mansions, The Net (Bi), The Hairy Head (Mao), The Stomach (Wei), Milky Way, Chinese Astronomy, White Tiger of the West</keywords>

# Star Chart for the Eleventh Lunar Month

->This chart illustrates the positions of the heavens during the peak of winter, identifying the constellations that serve as seasonal markers for the eleventh month.<-

### Temporal and Solar Positioning
In the eleventh month, the sun resides in the Dipper <note>original: 斗, *Dou*; the 8th lunar mansion, located in Sagittarius</note>. At dusk, the Eastern Wall <note>original: 東壁, *Dongbi*; the 14th lunar mansion</note> is at the meridian. At dawn, the Chariot <note>original: 軫, *Zhen*; the 28th lunar mansion</note> is at the meridian.

***

<margin>North Pole <gloss>original: 北極, *Beiji*; the pivot of the heavens around which all stars revolve.</gloss></margin>

<margin>Detached Palace <gloss>original: 離宮, *Ligong*; six stars in Pegasus representing a seasonal residence for the Emperor.</gloss></margin>

<margin>Flying Serpent <gloss>original: 騰蛇, *Tengshe*; a celestial serpent or dragon that flies among the clouds.</gloss></margin>

<margin>The Wall <gloss>original: 壁, *Bi*; the 14th lunar mansion, symbolizing the imperial archives or library walls.</gloss></margin>

<margin>The Encampment <gloss>original: 室, *Shi*; the 13th lunar mansion, representing the construction of the imperial palace.</gloss></margin>

<margin>Feathered Forest Army <gloss>original: 羽林軍, *Yulinjun*; a large group of 45 stars in Aquarius representing the Emperor’s elite guard.</gloss></margin>

<margin>Celestial Ramparts <gloss>original: 天壘城, *Tianleicheng*; a fortified wall in the sky used for defense.</gloss></margin>

<margin>Thunder and Lightning <gloss>original: 雷電, *Leidian*; associated with the transition of weather and celestial power.</gloss></margin>

<margin>Master of Earthworks <gloss>original: 土公, *Tugong*; a star responsible for overseeing construction and renovations.</gloss></margin>

<margin>North Gate of the Military Camp <gloss>original: 北落師門, *Beiluoshimen*; the bright star Fomalhaut, seen as a strategic gate to the celestial military barracks.</gloss></margin>

<margin>Eight Spirits <gloss>original: 八魁, *Bakui*; a net used for hunting or catching birds.</gloss></margin>

<margin>Axe and Hatchet <gloss>original: 鈇鉞, *Fuyue*; celestial weapons used for executions or justice.</gloss></margin>

<margin>Celestial Net <gloss>original: 天綱, *Tianguang*; the "mainstay" or guiding principle of the heavens.</gloss></margin>

<margin>Crying <gloss>original: 哭, *Ku*; an asterism associated with mourning and funerals.</gloss></margin>

<margin>Weeping <gloss>original: 泣, *Qi*; paired with "Crying," these stars relate to the somber aspects of the winter season.</gloss></margin>

<margin>Emptiness <gloss>original: 虛, *Xu*; the 11th lunar mansion, associated with ruins or mourning.</gloss></margin>

<margin>Rooftop <gloss>original: 危, *Wei*; the 12th lunar mansion, representing the peak of a building.</gloss></margin>

<margin>Tomb <gloss>original: 墳墓, *Fenmu*; located near the mansions of Emptiness and Rooftop, symbolizing burial.</gloss></margin>

<margin>Humans <gloss>original: 人, *Ren*; stars representing the common people and their role in the cosmic order.</gloss></margin>

<margin>Pestle <gloss>original: 杵, *Chu*; a tool for grinding grain.</gloss></margin>

<margin>Mortar <gloss>original: 臼, *Jiu*; paired with the Pestle for agricultural processing.</gloss></margin>

<margin>Gourd <gloss>original: 瓠瓜, *Hugua*; representing the harvest of melons and gourds.</gloss></margin>

<margin>Decayed Mortar <gloss>original: 敗臼, *Baijiu*; a broken or old mortar, suggesting the end of the harvest cycle.</gloss></margin>

# General Diagram of the Northern and Southern Constellations

->A representation of the celestial bureaucracy as mirrored in the heavens, showing the Purple Forbidden Enclosure and the surrounding mansions.<-

### The Central and Northern Regions
**The Purple Forbidden Enclosure** <note>original: 紫微垣, *Ziwei Yuan*; the central palace of the Emperor in the sky, surrounding the North Star.</note>
**The Extremely Venerable** <note>original: 太尊, *Taizun*; stars within the Big Dipper region representing high-ranking officials.</note>
**Celestial Law** <note>original: 天理, *Tianli*; a group of stars used to monitor the administration of justice on Earth.</note>
**The Northern Dipper** <note>original: 北斗, *Beidou*; known in the West as the Big Dipper or Plough, it serves as the celestial clock.</note>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page analyzes the astronomical discrepancies between two foundational Chinese classics—the Book of Documents (Shujing) and the Monthly Ordinances (Yueling)—specifically regarding which stars cross the meridian (culminate) during the middle of each season. It reconciles these differences by noting the specific timing of observations within the lunar month.</meta>

The *Book of Documents* records that in **mid-spring** the "Bird" star culminates, whereas the *Monthly Ordinances* <note>The "Monthly Ordinances" (Yueling) is a classic calendar of ritual and natural observations, often found as a chapter in the *Book of Rites*.</note> cites the "Bow" star. In **mid-summer**, the *Book* mentions the "Heart" star, while the *Monthly Ordinances* cites the "Neck" star. In **mid-autumn**, the *Book* mentions the "Emptiness" star, while the *Monthly Ordinances* cites the "Dipper" star. In **mid-winter**, the *Book* mentions the "Pleiades," while the *Monthly Ordinances* cites the "Net" star.

Thus, the <term>culminating stars (Zhongxing)</term> <note>These are stars that cross the observer's local meridian, marking the highest point in their path across the sky at a specific time, such as dusk or dawn.</note> in the *Book of Documents* are always "behind," while the culminating stars in the *Monthly Ordinances* are always "ahead." This is because the *Monthly Ordinances* records the stars based on the beginning of the month, whereas the *Book* records them based on the middle of the month. The morning and evening culminating stars in the *Monthly Ordinances* sometimes represent the <term>New Moon energy (Shuoqi)</term> <note>The start of the lunar month.</note> and sometimes the <term>Mid-month energy (Zhongqi)</term> <note>The astronomical center of a solar term.</note>. This is similar to how the *Book* refers to the "Seven Stars" <note>Specifically the 'Star' mansion within the Vermilion Bird constellation.</note>, where it sometimes notes their brightness and sometimes their appearance at dusk; they are all chosen simply because they are easy for the eye to see.

The morning and evening culminating stars for the four seasons in the *Monthly Ordinances* all align with the degrees of the <term>Twenty-Eight Lunar Mansions</term> <note>The 28 "stations" or constellations along the celestial equator used in ancient Chinese astronomy to track the movement of the moon and planets.</note>. However, in the month of mid-spring, it is said that the **Bow** star culminates at dusk and the **Establishment** star culminates at dawn. Now, these two stars—the Bow and the Establishment—are not actually included in the standard list of the Twenty-Eight Mansions. The reason the *Monthly Ordinances* mentions them anyway is that the **Bow** belongs to the <term>Eastern Well (Dongjing)</term> mansion and the **Establishment** belongs to the <term>Southern Dipper (Nandou)</term> mansion. 

Astronomical manuals record morning and evening culminations simply to provide people with a "center" (a reference point on the meridian). For example, in the month of mid-spring, the **Eastern Well** is at the center at dusk and the **Southern Dipper** is at the center at dawn. However, when examining the twenty-eight...

<summary>This text explains why the Book of Documents and the Monthly Ordinances name different stars for the same seasons, attributing the variations to whether the observations were made at the start or the middle of the lunar month. It also clarifies that certain stars mentioned outside the 28 Mansions are actually components of those major constellations.</summary>

<keywords>Book of Documents, Monthly Ordinances, Twenty-Eight Lunar Mansions, Culminating Stars, Mid-spring, Eastern Well, Southern Dipper, Chinese Astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical reconciliation of astronomical data between the "Monthly Ordinances" (Yueling) and the "Book of Documents" (Shangshu). It specifically addresses why certain representative stars, like the Bow and Establishment stars, were used in the Qin dynasty calendar to denote the culmination of much larger lunar mansions.</meta>

The <term>lunar mansions</term> <note>These are 28 divisions of the sky along the celestial equator, used in ancient Chinese astronomy to track the movement of the moon and the seasons.</note> of the **Eastern Well** and the **Southern Dipper** have the widest spans in terms of their degrees. When determining which stars culminate at dusk and dawn, although the mathematical degrees of the "Well" and "Dipper" may be central, the actual stars that make up these constellations are located quite far from the center of those degrees.

Therefore, when the Qin dynasty created its calendar, they were concerned that using the names "Well" and "Dipper" would not clearly convey the concept of the "middle" to the people. Regarding the degrees of the **Well** mansion, nothing is more clearly or brightly situated in the center than the **Bow star**; thus, they chose the Bow star to represent the Well. Looking at the degrees of the **Dipper**, nothing is more clearly central than the **Establishment star**; thus, they chose it to represent the Dipper. In reality, the Bow star belongs to the Well mansion, and the Establishment star belongs to the Dipper mansion.

**Jia Jian** <note>Likely a reference to the Han scholar Jia Kui or a related commentator on the classics.</note> said: "The Establishment star is effectively the Dipper star," meaning the degrees of Establishment are the degrees of the Dipper. This interpretation is correct. However, **Li Qi** <note>A Han dynasty commentator known for his work on the *Book of Han*.</note> claimed that in ancient times the Establishment star was the lunar mansion, and today the **Ox** mansion is used instead; this is a great mistake.

The *Monthly Ordinances* states: "In the second month of autumn, at dusk, the **Bow star** culminates." Note that the "Bird" star mentioned in the *Book of Documents* as culminating on the equinox is different from this. However, according to the interpretation of **Zheng Kangcheng** <note>Zheng Xuan (127–200 CE), one of the most influential commentators on the Chinese classics.</note>, the seven mansions of the South are collectively referred to as the "Bird" star. Since the **Well** and **Ghost** mansions are specific divisions of the "Bird" star, we know that the "Bird" star mentioned there refers to the same phenomenon.

Furthermore, in mid-summer at dusk, the **Neck** mansion culminates, whereas the *Book of Documents* states: "On the longest day, the star **Fire** culminates." Regarding this discrepancy, according to Zheng...

<summary>This page explains the substitution of specific stars (Bow and Establishment) for larger mansions in the Qin calendar to provide clearer visual markers for the meridian. It also uses Zheng Xuan's commentaries to reconcile conflicting astronomical records in the "Book of Documents" and the "Monthly Ordinances."</summary>
<keywords>Eastern Well, Southern Dipper, Bow Star, Establishment Star, Qin Dynasty Calendar, Zheng Xuan, Book of Documents, Monthly Ordinances, Culmination, Lunar Mansions</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical reconciliation of astronomical data between the "Monthly Ordinances" (Yueling) and the "Book of Documents" (Shangshu). The previous page discussed why the Qin dynasty calendar used representative stars like the Bow and Establishment stars to signify the culmination of the Well and Dipper mansions. This page shifts to address discrepancies between texts regarding the specific timing of these observations within the lunar month.</meta>

**Gu Sunhao** says: The term "Star of Fire" does not refer to the **Heart** mansion. The thirty degrees associated with the **Pleiades** <note>original: 昴 (*Mao*)</note> are collectively known as the **Great Fire** <note>original: 大火 (*Dahuo*), one of the twelve Jupiter stations in the ancient Chinese zodiac.</note>. Regarding the stars within the station of the Great Fire, the *Monthly Ordinances* cites the position at the **new moon** <note>The very beginning of the month.</note>, whereas the *Book of Documents* provides a general observation for the beginning and middle of the whole month. It is for this reason that the two texts do not match.

According to the *Monthly Ordinances*, in the mid-autumn month at dusk, the **Ox** mansion culminates in the center; yet the *Book of Documents* states: "At the equinox, the star is **Void**." In the mid-winter month, the *Monthly Ordinances* says the **East Wall** culminates, while the *Book of Documents* <gloss>says</gloss> the days are "at their shortest and the star is **Pleiades**." These discrepancies also stem from the fact that the *Monthly Ordinances* refers to the new moon, while the *Book of Documents* refers generally to the first and middle parts of the month; therefore, the underlying logic is not actually different.

In his commentary on the *Book of Documents*, **Kong Anguo** <note>A prominent Han dynasty scholar known for his "Old Text" recension of the classics.</note> notes that wherever "heat" is mentioned, it indicates that the **Seven Stars** are all visible. This refers to the general appearance of the constellations rather than their specific culmination at the southern meridian.

***

### Note on the Culminating Stars of the Twelve Months

If we take the "Four Midpoint Stars" from the "Canon of Yao" <note>A foundational astronomical chapter in the *Book of Documents* (*Shangshu*).</note> as a standard for the stars that culminate at dusk throughout the twelve months: the Classic states, "On the day of the equinox, the star is **Bird**." This means that among the seven mansions of the **Southern Vermilion Bird**—which are **Well**, **Ghost**, **Willow**, **Star**, **Extended Net**, **Wings**, and **Chariot**—the **Star** mansion is positioned precisely at the southern meridian <note>The "Wu" position, or the highest point in the sky due south.</note>.

However, when we come to **Lü Buwei’s** *Monthly Ordinances* <note>A third-century BCE text that synchronizes rituals with astronomical events.</note>, it states: "In mid-spring, at dusk, the **Bow** star culminates." By using the Bow star for comparison...

<summary>This page explains discrepancies in astronomical records between the "Monthly Ordinances" and the "Book of Documents," attributing differences to whether observations were made at the new moon or averaged across the month. It also lists the seven mansions of the Southern Vermilion Bird used to determine the spring equinox.</summary>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of how star positions have shifted over time, creating a discrepancy between the observations in the "Monthly Ordinances" and the "Book of Documents." The previous page established that the "Star of Fire" refers to a specific celestial station and noted that different texts observed stars at different points in the lunar month.</meta>

The **Bow** star <note>original: 弧 (*Hu*), an asterism associated with the Well mansion.</note> is located fifteen degrees away from the **Well** mansion. If we move another twenty degrees, we reach the second degree of the **Ghost** mansion; adding the fourteen degrees of the **Willow** mansion results in a total discrepancy of more than thirty degrees. 

Since a discrepancy already exists for the second month of spring <note>original: 仲春 (*Zhongchun*)</note>, then the **Star of Fire** in mid-summer, the **Star of the Void** in mid-autumn, and the **Star of the Pleiades** in mid-winter all likewise differ by more than thirty degrees. 

From this perspective, the **Bird** <note>The "Vermilion Bird," a collective name for the southern mansions associated with the spring season.</note> recedes into the third month, the **Fire** recedes into the sixth month, the **Void** recedes into the ninth month, and the **Pleiades** recede into the twelfth month. I have now taken the observation of **Orion** <note>original: 參 (*Shen*)</note> culminating at dusk in the first month as a baseline to examine the degrees of the <term>Twenty-Eight Mansions</term> <note>The 28 sectors of the sky used in ancient Chinese astronomy to track the movement of the moon and stars.</note> month by month. I have constructed the following chart to highlight those instances where the discrepancies are most significant, for the sake of easy reference and viewing.

<summary>This page calculates a cumulative shift of roughly thirty degrees (about one month) in celestial observations between ancient records and the author's time. It explains that major seasonal stars like Antares (Fire) and the Pleiades now appear to "recede" into later months than previously documented.</summary>

<keywords>Twenty-Eight Mansions, precession, Orion, Pleiades, Antares, Ghost mansion, Willow mansion, astronomical discrepancy, celestial mapping</keywords>

# Culminating Stars of the Spring and Summer Seasons

->The culminating stars <term>culminating stars (zhong xing): stars that reach their highest point in the sky on the observer's meridian</term> are situated between the South Pole and the North Pole.<-

***

->**SPRING**<-
<note>original: 春 (*Chun*)</note>

->**The Second Month**<-
<note>The second lunar month typically contains the Spring Equinox.</note>
At **Dusk**, the **Bow** constellation culminates.
<note>original: 昏弧 (*Hun hu*). The "Bow" is part of the Well mansion in the southern sky.</note>
At **Dawn**, the **Establishment** constellation culminates.
<note>original: 曉建 (*Xiao jian*). The "Establishment" is part of the Dipper mansion.</note>
The **Sun** is positioned in the **Legs** mansion.
<note>original: 中日在奎 (*Zhong ri zai kui*). The Legs (Kui) is the first mansion of the Western White Tiger.</note>

->**The Fourth Month**<-
<note>Associated stars: **Willow** (栁), **Star** (星), and **Ox** (牛).</note>

->**Associated Mansions**<-
**Neck** (亢), **Root** (氐), **Tail** (尾), **House** (室)
<note>These represent various lunar mansions across the eastern and northern quadrants of the sky.</note>

***

->**SUMMER**<-
<note>original: 夏 (*Xia*)</note>

->**The Fifth Month**<-
<note>The fifth lunar month typically contains the Summer Solstice.</note>
At **Dusk**, the **Neck** constellation culminates.
<note>original: 昏亢 (*Hun kang*). The Neck is the second mansion of the Eastern Dragon.</note>
At **Dawn**, the **Rooftop** constellation culminates.
<note>original: 曉危 (*Xiao wei*). The Rooftop (Wei) is part of the Northern Black Tortoise.</note>
The **Sun** is positioned in the **Willow** mansion.
<note>original: 中日在栁 (*Zhong ri zai liu*). The Willow (Liu) is the first mansion of the Southern Vermilion Bird.</note>

->**Associated Mansions and Positions**<-
**Stomach** (胃), **Wings** (翼), **Girl** (女), **Net** (畢)
<note>These stars reach prominence as the season progresses toward autumn.</note>

->**Alignments**<-
**Heart** (心), **Legs** (奎), **Looking Up** (仰)
<note>The term "Looking Up" likely refers to the observation of the zenith during the high sun of summer.</note>

# Culminating Stars of the Autumn and Winter Seasons

->**The Seventh, Eighth, and Ninth Months**<-
<note>These months correspond to the transition from the late summer heat to the first frosts of winter.</note>

# Sutra of the Morning and Evening Stars
<note>original: 明昏星經 (*Minghun Xingjing*). This title refers to a manual used to determine the time and season by observing which stars are "culminating" (reaching their highest point) at dawn and dusk.</note>

<image-desc>The diagram is a fan-shaped star chart. At the top, constellations are represented by small circles (stars) connected by lines. Below this, radiating lines of text provide the astronomical data for each of the twelve months, linking the time of day to the movement of the heavens.</image-desc>

<margin>
**The Path of the Sun and Moon**
<note>This margin tracks the monthly movement of the sun and moon through the zodiacal signs.</note>
1st Month: Today at dawn, <unclear>Li Yuan</unclear>; Sun and Moon are in the **Willow**.
2nd Month: Today at dawn, the **Archery Bow**; Sun and Moon are in the **Extended Net**.
3rd Month: Today at dawn, the **Great Brightness**; Sun and Moon are in the **Wings**.
4th Month: Today at dawn, the **Horn**; Sun and Moon are in the **Neck**.
5th Month: Today at dawn, the **Root**; Sun and Moon are in the **Room**.
6th Month: Today at dawn, the **Tail**; Sun and Moon are in the **Winnowing Basket**.
7th Month: Today at dawn, the **Dipper**; Sun and Moon are in the **Ox**.
8th Month: Today at dawn, the **Maiden**; Sun and Moon are in the **Emptiness**.
9th Month: Today at dawn, the **Rooftop**; Sun and Moon are in the **Encampment**.
10th Month: Today at dawn, the **Wall**; Sun and Moon are in the **Legs**.
11th Month: Today at dawn, the **Bond**; Sun and Moon are in the **Stomach**.
12th Month: Today at dawn, the **Hairy Head** <note>The Pleiades</note>; Sun and Moon are in the **Net**.

# Diagram of Successive Appearances
<note>original: 迭見之圖 (*Diejian zhi tu*). This diagram illustrates how different constellations "successively" appear and reach the meridian (the highest point in the sky) as the seasons change.</note>

<image-desc>A circular astronomical diagram. At the center is an arc representing the horizon or celestial meridian. Surrounding this center are various star clusters (constellations) depicted as small circles connected by lines. Radiating outward from the center are columns of text that align with twelve positions, corresponding to the months of the year and the hours of the day.</image-desc>

<margin>
**Monthly Ordinances:** At the Summer Solstice, at dawn, the **Weaving Maid** <note>original: 織女 (*Zhinu*); the star Vega</note> is at the center of the sky.
</margin>

(Ox) <note>Chou</note>
(Rat) <note>Zi</note>
(Boar) <note>Hai</note>
(Dog) <note>Xu</note>
(Rooster) <note>You</note>
(Monkey) <note>Shen</note>
(Goat) <note>Wei</note>
(Horse) <note>Wu</note>
(Snake) <note>Si</note>
(Dragon) <note>Chen</note>
(Rabbit) <note>Mao</note>
(Tiger) <note>Yin</note>
<note>These twelve characters are the Earthly Branches, used here to mark the twelve directions of the compass and the twelve double-hours of the day.</note>

> In the **First Month**, currently called the **Beginning of Spring**: the Moon is in the **Rooftop**; at dusk, **Orion** culminates; at dawn, the **Tail** culminates.
>
> In the **Second Month**, currently called the **Middle of Spring**: the Moon is in the **Legs**; at dusk, the **Bow** culminates; at dawn, the **Establishment Star** culminates.
>
> In the **Third Month**, currently called the **End of Spring**: the Moon is in the **Stomach**; at dusk, the **Seven Stars** <note>part of the Hydra constellation</note> culminate; at dawn, the **Ox-Boy** culminates.
>
> In the **Fourth Month**, currently called the **Beginning of Summer**: the Moon is in the **Net**; at dusk, the **Wings** culminate; at dawn, the **Maiden** culminates.
>
> In the **Fifth Month**, currently called the **Middle of Summer**: the Moon is in the **Eastern Well**; at dusk, the **Neck** culminates; at dawn, the **Rooftop** culminates.
>
> In the **Sixth Month**, currently called the **End of Summer**: the Moon is in the **Willow**; at dusk, the **Fire Star** <note>original: 火 (*Huo*); the red star Antares</note> culminates; at dawn, the **Legs** culminate.
>
> In the **Seventh Month**, currently called the **Beginning of Autumn**: the Moon is in the **Wings**; at dusk, the **Establishment Star** culminates; at dawn, the **Net** culminates.
>
> In the **Eighth Month**, currently called the **Middle of Autumn**: the Moon is in the **Horn**; at dusk, the **Ox-Boy** culminates; at dawn, the **Turtle Beak** culminates.
>
> In the **Nine Month**, currently called the **End of Autumn**: the Moon is in the **Room**; at dusk, the **Emptiness** culminates; at dawn, the **Willow** culminates.
>
> In the **Tenth Month**, currently called the **Beginning of Winter**: the Moon is in the **Tail**; at dusk, the **Rooftop** culminates; at dawn, the **Seven Stars** culminate.
>
> In the **Eleventh Month**, currently called the **Middle of Winter**: the Moon is in the **Dipper**; at dusk, the **Eastern Wall** culminates; at dawn, the **Chariot** culminates.
>
> In the **Twelfth Month**, currently called the **End of Winter**: the Moon is in the **Maiden**; at dusk, the **Bond** culminates; at dawn, the **Root** culminates.

<note>In this context, "culminates" (original: 中, *zhong*) means the star has reached the meridian, the imaginary line running from North to South through the zenith, marking its highest point in the sky for that night.</note>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on ancient Chinese astronomical systems, specifically focusing on the transition and interpretation of the Twenty-Eight Mansions across different classical texts such as the Book of Documents and the Monthly Ordinances.</meta>

The Commentary on the **Canon of Yao** <note>original: 堯典 *Yao Dian*, a foundational chapter in the *Book of Documents*</note> states that the **twenty-eight mansions** <term>Twenty-eight Mansions: A system of constellations along the celestial equator used as markers for the moon's progress</term> rotate along with the movement of the heavens, taking turns appearing in the southern sky. Each month, specific stars reach the "middle" <note>the meridian or highest point in the sky</note>.

The fourth section of **The Great Plan** <note>original: 洪範 *Hong Fan*</note> mentions "the stars and constellations." The annotations explain: "The twenty-eight mansions appear in succession to order the **solar terms** <term>Solar Terms: 24 points in the traditional calendar that track the sun's position and seasonal changes</term>." The commentary further notes that these mansions appear alternately at dusk and dawn. Just as the **Monthly Ordinances** <note>original: 月令 *Yue Ling*, a ritual calendar from the *Book of Rites*</note> records the stars reaching the middle of the sky at dusk and dawn for all twelve months, this system serves to arrange the seasonal timing.

The *Astronomical Treatise of the Book of Sui* <note>original: 隋書天文志 *Sui Shu Tianwen Zhi*</note> states: "The legendary sovereign Fuxi <note>original: 庖犧氏 *Pao Xi Shi*</note> looked up to observe the heavens and down to examine the earth. He used the twenty-eight mansions of heaven to circle the degrees of the celestial vault and fixed them to the twelve positions." Those stars that rotate with the heavens are called **fixed stars** <note>original: 經星 *Jing Xing*</note>.

Regarding the stars seen in succession during the four mid-season months in the *Canon of Yao*, the alignment was centered on the position of *Wu* <note>The "Horse" position in the Chinese zodiac, representing due South or the solar noon</note> as the standard. However, the stars currently seen at dusk and dawn are considered to "middle" when they reach the position of *Wei* <note>The "Goat" position, representing South-Southwest or the time between 1 and 3 PM</note>. 

Generally, the movement of the stars and constellations begins with their appearance at the position of *Chen* <note>The "Dragon" position, East-Southeast</note> and ends with their setting or hiding at *Xu* <note>The "Dog" position, West-Northwest</note>. From *Chen* to *Xu*, the stars are "aligned" at *Wu* and reach their "middle" at *Wei*. This is why the *Canon of Yao* says that when the day is at its longest, the **Fire Star** <term>Fire Star: The star Antares (Dahuo), used as a primary seasonal marker in ancient China</term> aligns with midsummer; this uses the *Wu* position as the point of alignment. By the time of the *Monthly Ordinances*, reaching the end of summer, it says "at dusk the Fire Star culminates," which uses *Wei* as the central point. The **Zuo Commentary** <note>original: 左傳 *Zuo Zhuan*</note>...

<summary>This page explains the astronomical calculations used to determine the seasons in ancient China, comparing different methods of tracking the "Twenty-Eight Mansions" across various classical texts. It specifically addresses how the culminating position of the Fire Star (Antares) shifted in description between the Canon of Yao and later ritual texts.</summary>
<keywords>Twenty-eight Mansions, Canon of Yao, Fire Star, Antares, Chinese Astronomy, Earthly Branches, Solar Terms, Monthly Ordinances</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of how celestial bodies—specifically the Sun and the "Fire Star" (Antares)—reaching the meridian (the "middle" of the sky) serve as indicators for the transition of seasons, citing the Classic of Poetry to support these ancient astronomical observations.</meta>

...when the Sun and the **Fire Star** <note>original: 火星 *Huo Xing*; here referring to the star Antares in the Heart mansion, used traditionally to track the arrival of summer and its subsequent passing</note> reach the middle of the sky, the cold and heat recede.

The **Classic of Poetry** <note>original: 詩 *Shi*, specifically referencing the "Odes of Yong"</note> says: "When the **Ding** mansion <note>original: 定 *Ding*; the Encampment mansion, which reaches the meridian at dusk during the tenth lunar month</note> was just in the middle." This also considers the position of **Wei** <note>original: 未; the eighth Earthly Branch, representing the direction south-southwest or the time between 1:00 PM and 3:00 PM</note> to be the "middle."

<summary>This passage explains how the meridian transit of specific stars like Antares and the Encampment mansion signals seasonal changes and the proper time for activities, supported by classical poetic authority.</summary>
<keywords>Fire Star, Antares, Classic of Poetry, Ding mansion, Encampment mansion, meridian transit, Wei branch, seasonal transitions</keywords>

# Diagram of the Jade Sighting-Tube Pointing to the Seasons According to the Flow of Vital Energy

->[Central Image: A celestial map showing the **Big Dipper** <note>original: 北斗 *Beidou*</note> at the center. Its handle, specifically the star known as the **Jade Sighting-Tube** <note>original: 玉衡 *Yuheng*; Alioth, the fifth star of the Big Dipper, often used to represent the pointing mechanism of the constellation</note>, extends outward to act as a hand on a clock, pointing toward the encircling seasons and directions.]<-

This diagram illustrates the "Monthly Establishment" <note>original: 建 *Jian*; the directional point on the horizon where the handle of the Big Dipper points at dusk, marking the beginning of a solar month</note>. As the Earth rotates and orbits, the handle of the Dipper appears to sweep through the sky, signaling the transition of **Vital Energy** <note>original: 炁 *Qi*; the seasonal breath or energy that changes throughout the year</note> and the arrival of agricultural milestones.

### The Correlation of Directions and Seasons

The following table represents the information contained in the two concentric rings of the diagram, moving clockwise from the bottom (North/Winter):

| Compass Direction (Stems & Branches) | Solar Term | Seasonal Significance |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Zi** (North) | **Winter Solstice** | The height of Winter; days begin to lengthen. |
| **Gui** | **Slight Cold** | The onset of the coldest period. |
| **Chou** | **Great Cold** | The peak of the year's cold. |
| **Gen** (Northeast) | **Beginning of Spring** | The start of the astrological year and Spring. |
| **Yin** | **Rain Water** | Ice melts and rainfall increases. |
| **Jia** | **Awakening of Insects** | Thunder rouses hibernating insects. |
| **Mao** (East) | **Spring Equinox** | Day and night are of equal length. |
| **Yi** | **Pure Brightness** | Time for tending graves and enjoying the outdoors. |
| **Chen** | **Grain Rain** | Rain falls to help the early crops grow. |
| **Xun** (Southeast) | **Beginning of Summer** | The start of the Summer season. |
| **Si** | **Grain Buds** | Grain seeds begin to plump. |
| **Bing** | **Grain in Ear** | Barley and wheat are ready for harvest. |
| **Wu** (South) | **Summer Solstice** | The longest day of the year; yang energy peaks. |
| **Ding** | **Slight Heat** | The weather begins to grow hot. |
| **Wei** | **Great Heat** | The hottest period of the summer. |
| **Kun** (Southwest) | **Beginning of Autumn** | The start of the Autumn season. |
| **Shen** | **End of Heat** | The heat of summer begins to fade. |
| **Geng** | **White Dew** | Dew begins to form as the air cools. |
| **You** (West) | **Autumn Equinox** | Day and night are equal; harvest is in full swing. |
| **Xin** | **Cold Dew** | Dew becomes colder and more frequent. |
| **Xu** | **Frost Descent** | The first appearance of frost on the ground. |
| **Qian** (Northwest) | **Beginning of Winter** | The start of the Winter season. |
| **Hai** | **Slight Snow** | The first light dusting of snow appears. |
| **Ren** | **Great Snow** | Snow begins to accumulate heavily. |

<margin>The handle of the Dipper points to the **Zi** direction at the **Winter Solstice**, marking the return of the light.</margin>

<note>The diagram combines the **Twelve Earthly Branches** (the 12 animals/hours), the **Ten Heavenly Stems** (minus the center), and the **Four Trigrams** (Gen, Xun, Kun, Qian) to create a 24-point compass. This allows for precise measurement of the 24 solar terms, which occur roughly every 15 days.</note>

<summary>This diagram visualizes the Big Dipper as a celestial clock, where the star Alioth (the "Jade Sighting-Tube") points to specific compass directions to mark the 24 solar terms of the agricultural year.</summary>
<keywords>Big Dipper, Yuheng, Alioth, 24 Solar Terms, Heavenly Stems, Earthly Branches, Winter Solstice, Summer Solstice, Celestial Compass, Vital Energy, Qi</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This text details the correlation between the "Jade Balance" (a star in the Big Dipper) and the 24 solar terms of the agricultural calendar. It uses the twenty-four directions of the traditional Chinese compass (composed of Earthly Branches, Celestial Stems, and Trigrams) to map the celestial movement across a full year.</meta>

The <term>Esoteric Interpretation of the Classic of Filial Piety</term> <note>original: 孝經緯 (Xiaojing Wei). These are apocryphal texts from the Han Dynasty that interpret Confucian classics through a mystical and astronomical lens</note> states: After **Major Snow** <note>Daxue, approximately Dec 7</note>, the **Jade Balance** <note>original: 玉衡 (Yuheng); the fifth star in the Big Dipper constellation, also known as Alioth</note> points to the <term>Earthly Branch of Zi</term> <note>Due North</note>. At the **Winter Solstice**, it points to the <term>Celestial Stem of Gui</term> <note>North-Northwest</note>. At **Minor Cold**, it points to the <term>Earthly Branch of Chou</term> <note>North-Northeast</note>. At **Major Cold**, it points to the <term>Trigram of Gen</term> <note>Northeast</note>.

At the **Beginning of Spring**, it points to the <term>Earthly Branch of Yin</term> <note>East-Northeast</note>. At **Rain Water**, it points to the <term>Celestial Stem of Jia</term> <note>East-Northeast</note>. At the **Awakening of Insects**, it points to the <term>Earthly Branch of Mao</term> <note>Due East</note>. At the **Vernal Equinox**, it points to the <term>Celestial Stem of Yi</term> <note>East-Southeast</note>. At **Bright and Clear**, it points to the <term>Earthly Branch of Chen</term> <note>East-Southeast</note>. At **Grain Rain**, it points to the <term>Trigram of Xun</term> <note>Southeast</note>.

At the **Beginning of Summer**, it points to the <term>Earthly Branch of Si</term> <note>South-Southeast</note>. At **Grain Buds**, it points to the <term>Celestial Stem of Bing</term> <note>South-Southeast</note>. At **Grain in Ear**, it points to the <term>Earthly Branch of Wu</term> <note>Due South</note>. At the **Summer Solstice**, it points to the <term>Celestial Stem of Ding</term> <note>South-Southwest</note>. At **Minor Heat**, it points to the <term>Earthly Branch of Wei</term> <note>South-Southwest</note>. At **Major Heat**, it points to the <term>Trigram of Kun</term> <note>Southwest</note>.

At the **Beginning of Autumn**, it points to the <term>Earthly Branch of Shen</term> <note>West-Southwest</note>. At **End of Heat**, it points to the <term>Celestial Stem of Geng</term> <note>West-Southwest</note>. At **White Dew**, it points to the <term>Earthly Branch of You</term> <note>Due West</note>. At the **Autumnal Equinox**, it points to the <term>Celestial Stem of Xin</term> <note>West-Northwest</note>. At **Cold Dew**, it points to the <term>Earthly Branch of Xu</term> <note>West-Northwest</note>. At **Frost's Descent**, it points to the <term>Trigram of Qian</term> <note>Northwest</note>.

At the **Beginning of Winter**, it points to the <term>Earthly Branch of Hai</term> <note>North-Northwest</note>. At **Minor Snow**, it points to the <term>Celestial Stem of Ren</term> <note>North-Northwest</note>. Regarding Major Snow, looking into the **Treatise of the Han Dynasty** <note>original: 漢志 (Hanzhi); refers to the astronomical records in the Book of Han</note>, it says the **Jade Balance** refers to the **Big Dipper**.

<summary>This passage describes an ancient astronomical "clock," explaining how the position of the star Alioth in the Big Dipper marks each of the 24 solar terms throughout the year. It aligns celestial movement with the traditional 24-direction compass system used in ancient China.</summary>
<keywords>Jade Balance, Big Dipper, 24 Solar Terms, Chinese Astronomy, Earthly Branches, Celestial Stems, Book of Han, Xiaojing Wei</keywords>

# Diagram of the Movement of the Star Zhaoyao Through the Months

<note>This circular diagram functions as a celestial clock. In ancient Chinese astronomy, the "handle" of the Big Dipper was seen as a pointer. The star Zhaoyao (Gamma Boötis), which sits at the very tip of an extended line from the handle, was used to determine the start of months and seasons based on which direction (represented by the Earthly Branches) it pointed toward at dusk.</note>

| <term>Earthly Branch</term> <note>Directional markers</note> | Seasonal Month | <term>Star Alignment</term> |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Wu** <note>South</note> | **Mid-summer** | The star Zhaoyao points here |
| **Wei** <note>South-Southwest</note> | **Late summer** | The star Zhaoyao points here |
| **Shen** <note>West-Southwest</note> | **Early autumn** | The star Zhaoyao points here |
| **You** <note>West</note> | **Mid-autumn** | The star Zhaoyao points here |
| **Xu** <note>West-Northwest</note> | **Late autumn** | The star Zhaoyao points here |
| **Hai** <note>North-Northwest</note> | **Early winter** | The star Zhaoyao points here |
| **Zi** <note>North</note> | **Mid-winter** | The star Zhaoyao points here |
| **Chou** <note>North-Northeast</note> | **Late winter** | The star Zhaoyao points here |
| **Yin** <note>East-Northeast</note> | **Early spring** | The star Zhaoyao points here |
| **Mao** <note>East</note> | **Mid-spring** | The star Zhaoyao points here |
| **Chen** <note>East-Southeast</note> | **Late spring** | The star Zhaoyao points here |
| **Si** <note>South-Southeast</note> | **Early summer** | The star Zhaoyao points here |

### Central Celestial Figures
<note>These stars represent the "Great Chariot" or Big Dipper and its associated pointers.</note>

*   **Zhaoyao** <note>original: 招搖; Gamma Boötis, also known as the "Twinkling Indicator." It acts as the ultimate tip of the Dipper's handle</note>
*   **Genghe** <note>original: 梗河; The "Celestial Spear," a group of stars in the constellation Boötes</note>
*   **The Five Stars of the Dipper's Body:**
    *   **Quan** <note>original: 權; "The Balance Beam" or Megrez</note>
    *   **Heng** <note>original: 衡; "The Jade Balance" or Alioth</note>
    *   **Ji** <note>original: 璣; "The Pearl" or Phecda</note>
    *   **Xuan** <note>original: 璇; "The Jade Star" or Merak</note>
    *   **Shu** <note>original: 樞; "The Pivot" or Dubhe</note>

<note>By observing these stars at nightfall, an astronomer could determine the exact month of the year. For instance, when Zhaoyao points toward the "Yin" direction (East-Northeast), it signals the arrival of the first month of spring.</note>

<summary>This page features a diagnostic diagram showing how the star Zhaoyao—the tip of the Big Dipper’s extended handle—rotates through the twelve Earthly Branches to mark the transition of months and seasons. It serves as a visual guide for the traditional Chinese seasonal calendar based on stellar observation.</summary>

<keywords>Zhaoyao, Big Dipper, Earthly Branches, Sancai Tuhui, celestial observation, seasonal calendar, Boötes, Alioth, Dubhe</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>The text continues the discussion of astronomical observations and calendrical calculations, specifically focusing on the orientation of the Big Dipper's handle throughout the year.</meta>

In the ***Huainanzi*** <note>original: "淮南子"; a foundational 2nd-century BCE Chinese text covering philosophy, statecraft, and cosmology</note>, it is stated that: In the first month of spring, the **Zhaoyao** <note>original: "招搖"; the star Gamma Boötis, which was used as an extension of the Big Dipper’s handle</note> points to the direction of **Yin** <note>The third Earthly Branch, associated with the start of spring</note>; in the second month of spring, it points to **Mao**; and in the third month of spring, it points to **Chen**. 

In the first month of summer, it points to **Si**; in the second month of summer, it points to **Wu**; and in the third month of summer, it points to **Wei**. 

In the first month of autumn, it points to **Shen**; in the second month of autumn, it points to **You**; and in the third month of autumn, it points to **Xu**. 

In the first month of winter, it points to **Hai**; in the second month of winter, it points to **Zi**; and in the third month of winter, it points to **Chou** <note>These directions—Yin, Mao, Chen, etc.—correspond to the twelve Earthly Branches used to divide the horizon into a 360-degree circle</note>.

The ***Treatise of Han*** <note>original: "漢志"; referring to the astronomical records in the *Book of Han*</note> says: In an intercalary month <note>A "leap month" added to the lunar calendar to keep it in sync with the solar year</note>, there is no **Mid-Solar Term** <term>Zhongqi|中炁; one of the 12 solar terms that falls in the middle of a lunar month. If a month lacks one, it is designated as a leap month</term>. During these times, the Dipper points diagonally between two Earthly Branches.

The ***Treatise of Jin*** <note>original: "晉志"; referring to the astronomical records in the *Book of Jin*</note> says: The **Northern Dipper** is the <term>Pivot|樞機; the central mechanism or 'hinge' of the universe</term> of the **Seven Regulators** <term>Seven Regulators|七政; the Sun, the Moon, and the five visible planets: Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</term> and the primal source of Yin and Yang. It revolves in the center of the heavens and governs the four directions to establish the four seasons. The star **Fu** <note>original: "輔星"; the star Alcor</note> is attached to **Kaiyang** <note>original: "開陽"; the star Mizar, located in the handle of the Dipper</note>; it exists to assist the Dipper and bring its work to completion.

<summary>This page explains how ancient Chinese astronomers used the orientation of the Big Dipper (specifically the star Zhaoyao) to determine the months of the year and the four seasons. It cites classical historical treatises to describe the Dipper as the central celestial mechanism that regulates the planets and the calendar.</summary>

<keywords>Big Dipper, Huainanzi, Earthly Branches, Seven Regulators, astronomy, intercalary month, Alcor and Mizar, Chinese calendar</keywords>

# General Star Map of the "Great East" <note>original: "大東總星圖" (Dadong zongxing tu); "Great East" refers to a famous poem in the *Classic of Poetry* (Shijing) that mentions these specific stars and constellations</note>

At the center of the celestial sphere, we find the **Weaving Girl** <note>original: "織女" (Zhīnǚ); the star Vega</note> and her counterpart, the **Ox-Boy** <note>original: "牽牛" (Qiānniú); the star Altair</note>.

**To the West (marked at the right):**
The **Moon sets** <note>original: "月入" (yuèrù)</note> here. Nearby, Venus appears again, but as the **Evening Star** <note>original: "長庚夕見" (Chánggēng xījiàn); literally "The Long Path seen at dusk"</note>.

# Sancai Tuhui, Volume 3: Astronomy

The **Weaving Girl** <note>the star Vega in the constellation Lyra</note> is a celestial maiden, and the **Cowherd** <note>the star Altair in the constellation Aquila</note> is the one who drives the carriage <note>referring to the traditional folk tale where these two lovers are separated by the Silver River, or Milky Way</note>. **Qiming** <note>the "Morning Star"</note> and **Changgeng** <note>the "Evening Star"</note> are both the planet **Venus** <term>Jinxing; literally "the Metal Star," the second planet from the Sun</term>. Because it emerges before the sun rises, it is called Qiming; because it sets after the sun, it is called Changgeng.

The **Celestial Net** refers to the **Net mansion** <note>a constellation in the modern sign of Taurus</note>; its shape resembles a hand-net used for catching rabbits. The **Sieve** and the **Dipper** <note>referring here to the Southern Dipper in Sagittarius</note>—these two lunar mansions—are seen in the southern sky during the interval between summer and autumn. We speak of the **Northern Dipper** <note>the Big Dipper in Ursa Major</note> because of its position relative to the north of the Sieve constellation.

# Diagram of the Seventh Month’s Descending Fire

->**[Central Label]**<-
->**The Fire Star** <note>original: "火" (Huo); this refers to the "Great Fire" or **Antares** <term>Dahuo; the primary star of the Heart Mansion in the Azure Dragon constellation</term>, rather than the planet Mars.</note><-

| Month | Positional Observation |
| :--- | :--- |
| **1st Month** | |
| **2nd Month** | |
| **3rd Month** | |
| **4th Month** | |
| **5th Month** | **Culmination at dusk** <note>original: "昏中" (hunzhong); the star reaches its highest point in the sky as night begins.</note> |
| **6th Month** | Observed at **daybreak** <note>original: "昧爽" (meishuang); the faint light before the sun rises.</note> |
| **7th Month** | **Descending** <note>original: "流" (liu); literally "flowing." This indicates the star moving westward and downward toward the horizon, signaling the end of summer's peak.</note> |
| **8th Month** | At the **boundary** <note>original: "圉" (yu); suggests a transition point or a "stable" limit in the star's descent.</note> |
| **9th Month** | **Hidden at dusk** <note>original: "伏昏" (fuhun); the star is no longer visible in the evening sky as it sets with or before the sun.</note> |
| **10th Month** | |
| **11th Month** | **Culmination at dawn** <note>original: "旦中" (danzhong); the star reaches its highest point in the sky just as the sun is about to rise.</note> |
| **12th Month** | |

**Explanation of Terminology:**

The phrase **"In the seventh month, the Fire Star descends"** <note>original: "七月流火" (Qi yue liu huo)</note> is a famous line from the *Book of Odes* <note>the *Shijing*, the oldest existing collection of Chinese poetry</note>. To the ancient observer, the movement of the **Great Fire** <note>original: "大火" (Dahuo)</note>—also known as **Heart Mansion Two** <note>original: "心宿二" (Xinxiu Er)</note>—served as a celestial clock. When this bright red star began its westward "flow" in the seventh lunar month, it warned the people that the scorching heat of summer was passing and they must begin preparing clothes for the coming cold of autumn and winter.

The terms **dusk culmination** <note>original: "昏中" (hunzhong)</note> and **dawn culmination** <note>original: "旦中" (danzhong)</note> refer to the moments when the star crosses the local meridian (the highest point in the sky) at the beginning and end of the night, respectively. These observations allowed ancient astronomers to fix the solar terms and align the civil calendar with the actual seasons.

<summary>This page features a diagram tracking the annual movement of the star Antares, explaining the classic astronomical phrase "In the seventh month, the Fire Star descends" as a marker for the arrival of autumn.</summary>

<keywords>Antares (Fire Star), Heart Mansion, Book of Odes, Astronomy, Calendrical Diagram, Seventh Month, Sancai Tuhui</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
The Fire star is the "Great Fire," which is the "Heart" star <note>original: 心星 *Xinxing*; the central star of the "Heart" mansion in the Azure Dragon constellation, known in the West as Antares</note>. In the dusk of the sixth month, it reaches its highest point in the southern sky. By the dusk of the seventh month, it descends and flows westward <note>This "westward flow" is a famous seasonal marker from the *Book of Odes* signaling the end of summer and the coming of cooler weather</note>.

The Fire star remains hidden from the ninth through the tenth months; at dusk, it is not visible at all. Only after the Winter Solstice—and through the first, second, third, and fourth months—is it seen in the period following the sun <note>This refers to its visibility at dawn or dusk relative to the sun’s position during the transition of seasons</note>.

In the *Commentary of Zuo* <note>The *Zuo Zhuan*, an ancient Chinese narrative history and commentary on the *Spring and Autumn Annals*</note>, Zhang Hui said: "When the Fire star is at the meridian, the extremes of cold and heat retreat." Fu Qian’s commentary states: "When it reaches the meridian at dawn, the cold retreats; when the Fire star reaches the meridian at dusk, the heat retreats."

<summary>This page explains the astronomical movements of the star Antares (the "Fire Star") and its significance as a seasonal calendar marker in ancient Chinese agricultural and ritual life.</summary>
<keywords>Fire Star, Antares, Heart Mansion, Zuo Zhuan, astronomy, seasonal cycles, Book of Odes</keywords>

### Collected Illustrations of the Three Realms, Volume 3: Astronomy
Page 39

# Diagram of the Sun and Moon's Celestial Circuits

<image-desc>A diagram showing two circles representing the Sun (日) and the Moon (月) positioned above a series of vertical text columns explaining their celestial movements.</image-desc>

->Sun<- ->Moon<-

---

The essence of the **Great Yang** <note>original: 太陽 *Taiyang*; the Sun</note> follows the heavens, rotating to the left. The heavens travel one circuit every day, which is consistently 365 and 1/4 degrees <note>In traditional Chinese astronomy, a degree was defined by the Sun's daily motion, making a full circle 365.25 degrees to match the days in a year</note>, plus an additional degree. The Sun also completes one circuit in a single day, but compared to the movement of the heavens, it falls short by one degree. After a total of 365 and 1/4 days, it meets again with the heavens <note>this describes the completion of one solar year</note>.

The essence of the **Great Yin** <note>original: 太陰 *Taiyin*; the Moon</note> also follows the heavens, rotating to the left. In one day, it consistently falls short of the heavens by thirteen degrees and a fraction <note>original: 有奇 *you qi*; meaning "and a remainder" or "some more"</note>, and it falls short of the Sun’s position by twelve degrees and a fraction. After a total of twenty-seven days and a fraction, it meets with the heavens <note>a sidereal month, the time it takes the moon to return to the same place among the stars</note>. After a total of twenty-nine days and a fraction, it meets with the Sun <note>a synodic month, the time between two new moons</note>.

<note>technical terms: **Great Yang** (Sun), **Great Yin** (Moon), **Celestial Circuit** (the path around the sky), **Leftward Rotation** (the apparent motion of the stars), **Collected Illustrations of the Three Realms** (the title of this 1609 encyclopedia)</note>

<summary>This page illustrates and explains the relative orbital speeds of the Sun and Moon compared to the celestial sphere. It provides the mathematical duration for a solar year and both sidereal and synodic lunar months.</summary>

<keywords>Sun, Moon, celestial mechanics, solar year, lunar month, Great Yang, Great Yin, Sancai Tuhui, Chinese astronomy</keywords>
