# Diagram of the Celestial Cycles of the Five Planets

<image-desc>A circular cosmological diagram divided into five sections around a central circle, representing the five visible planets (the Five Stars) and their celestial cycles. Each section contains the name of the planet in a small circle and descriptive text regarding its orbital period and movement relative to the heavens.</image-desc>

<insert>
**Saturn** <term>original: 土 *Tu*; the Soil or Earth Star</term>
One name for this planet is the **Filling Star** <note>original: 填星 *Tianxing*; also translated as the "Exorcist" or "Presiding" star</note>. It leans against the heavens, rotating to the left, but does not keep pace with the heavens <note>this refers to the planet's slow "retrograde" or eastward movement against the background of the stars</note>. In its average motion, it shifts by one degree every twenty-eight days, moves through one zodiacal mansion <term>original: 宮 *gong*; one of the twelve divisions of the ecliptic</term> every twenty-eight months, and completes one full celestial circuit in twenty-eight years.
</insert>

<insert>
**Jupiter** <term>original: 木 *Mu*; the Wood Star</term>
One name for this planet is the **Year Star** <note>original: 歲星 *Suixing*; because its 12-year cycle corresponds to the twelve earthly branches</note>. It follows the heavens, rotating to the left, but does not keep pace with the heavens. Following its average speed, it shifts by one degree every twelve days, moves through one zodiacal mansion every year, and completes one full celestial circuit in twelve years.
</insert>

<insert>
**Mars** <term>original: 火 *Huo*; the Fire Star</term>
One name for this planet is the **Shimmering Delusion** <note>original: 熒惑 *Yinghuo*; a name inspired by its reddish glow and its seemingly erratic, "confusing" path through the sky</note>. It follows the heavens, rotating to the left, but does not keep pace with the heavens. It is consistently governed by the influence of the Sun <note>original: 太陽 *Taiyang*</note> every ten months. Its movement varies, appearing sometimes ahead and sometimes behind. It moves through one zodiacal mansion every two months and completes one full celestial circuit in two years.
</insert>

<insert>
**Venus** <term>original: 金 *Jin*; the Metal Star</term>
One name for this planet is the **Great White** <note>original: 太白 *Taibai*</note>. It follows the heavens, rotating to the left, but does not keep pace with the heavens. Appearing sometimes ahead and sometimes behind, it moves in accordance with the Sun. It moves through one zodiacal mansion every two months and completes one full celestial circuit in one year.
</insert>

<insert>
**Mercury** <term>original: 水 *Shui*; the Water Star</term>
One name for this planet is the **Hour Star** <note>original: 辰星 *Chenxing*; also known as the Chronos Star</note>. It follows the heavens, rotating to the left, but does not keep pace with the heavens. It assists the Sun in its travel, appearing sometimes ahead and sometimes behind. In its average motion, it shifts one degree every month <note>The text says "one degree per month," though Mercury's physical orbit is much faster; this likely refers to its appearance within the traditional Chinese calendar system</note>, moves through one zodiacal mansion every month, and completes one full celestial circuit in one year.
</insert>

<summary>This page illustrates the orbital periods and naming conventions of the five visible planets in traditional Chinese astronomy. It establishes a hierarchy of movement where planets "lag behind" the daily rotation of the heavens at specific, predictable intervals.</summary>
<keywords>Five Planets, celestial mechanics, Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Venus, Mercury, orbital period, zodiacal mansions, Sancai Tuhui</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the astrological and cosmological records, specifically focusing on the movements of the Sun and its role as a mirror for the morality of the state.</meta>

### Governance and Records of the Seven Luminaries

The Sun follows the Yellow Path <note>original: 黃道 (Huangdao), the ecliptic or the sun's apparent path across the sky</note>, moving eastward one degree every day and night. It completes a full circuit of the heavens in three hundred sixty-five and a fraction days. Its transit through the Eastern Lands is called spring; its transit through the Southern Lands is summer; its transit through the Western Lands is autumn; and its transit through the Northern Lands is winter. Through these movements, the cycles of **Yin and Yang** and the seasonal rhythms of cold and heat are established.

Therefore, the traditions say: the Sun is the essence of Great *Yang* <term>Taiyang: the supreme active, masculine, and solar principle in Chinese cosmology</term>. It presides over the nourishment of life and the bestowal of benevolent grace; it is the symbolic representation of the Sovereign. If the Sovereign has flaws, the Sun will inevitably reveal these hidden faults as a warning to him. 

For this reason, when the Sun shines upon a nation that possesses the "Way" <note>the *Dao*, or the proper moral and cosmic order</note>, its light is brilliant. The Sovereign enjoys auspicious prosperity, and the common people live in peace and tranquility. When a Sovereign occupies his rightful place and his governance achieves Great Peace <term>Taiping: a state of perfect social and cosmic harmony</term>, the Sun will manifest in five colors <note>an auspicious rainbow-like solar phenomenon</note>.

Any celestial anomalies or strange variations are entirely due to a deficiency in the Sovereign’s virtue; thus, the changes in the heavens correspond to his actions. If the Sun’s color changes and a halo appears, it signifies the defeat of an army. If there is no halo during a color change, it portends mourning and illness. If the Sovereign lacks virtue and his ministers plot rebellion, the Sun will turn red and lose its radiance. If the Sun’s proper color is lost, the nation over which it shines will not prosper, and the day will turn dark as it travels.

<summary>This page describes the solar cycle and its astrological significance, emphasizing the "Mandate of Heaven" where solar anomalies serve as direct reflections of a ruler's moral integrity and political stability.</summary>
<keywords>Sun, Ecliptic, Seven Luminaries, Sovereignty, Great Yang, Yin and Yang, Celestial Omens, Great Peace, Solar Halo</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on solar anomalies and their portents for the state, transitioning from solar halos to more severe solar phenomena before introducing the nature and phases of the Moon.</meta>

If people cast no shadows and this lasts until evening, it indicates that punishments from above are harsh and the people below find life unbearable; within a year, there will be great floods. If the Sun is dim during the day and flocks of birds cry out, the state has lost its proper governance. 

If a bird is seen within the Sun <note>This likely refers to sunspots, traditionally personified as a three-legged crow in Chinese mythology</note>, the Sovereign is unenlightened and his governance is in chaos. There will be a "Gathering of White Clothes" <note>original: 白衣之會 (baiyi zhi hui); white is the color of mourning, but also represents commoners or rebels who lack official rank</note>, and generals will set out with banners raised for war. 

If there are **sunspots** <term>heizi: literally "black seeds," the historical term for solar spots</term> within the Sun, it means ministers are usurping authority and monopolizing blessings, thereby harming the "Way of the Sovereign." 

If black vapors or black clouds appear—sometimes three, sometimes five—it is a sign of a minister deposing his lord. This represents **Yin encroaching upon Yang** <note>original: 陰侵陽 (yin qin yang)</note>, a symbol of a minister overshadowing the Sovereign. This portends the fall of a nation or the death of a ruler. If a solar eclipse occurs and stars become visible during the day, there will be the assassination of a ruler and great floods; the realm will be split asunder. In such times, the King should **cultivate his virtue** <note>original: 修德 (xiu de)</note> to perform the **rituals of averting calamity** <term>rang: a ritual intended to expel evil or prevent a predicted disaster</term>.

### The Nature of the Moon

The Moon is the essence of the **Great Yin** <term>Taiyin: the supreme passive, feminine, and lunar principle, the counterpart to the Sun</term>. Its shape is perfectly round and its substance is pure. When the sunlight shines upon it, we see its brightness. The parts where the sunlight does not reach are called the *po* <note>original: 魄 (po); in Chinese cosmology, this refers to the dark, receptive "soul" or the unlit portion of the lunar disk</note>. 

Therefore, on the day of the **Full Moon** <note>original: 望 (wang), the 15th day of the lunar month</note>, the Sun and the Moon face one another, and so its shape appears perfectly round to us. On the days of the **two quarter moons** <note>original: 二弦 (er xian), literally "the two bowstrings"</note>, the Sun shines upon its side, and thus it appears as a "half-darkness." On the days of the **new moon and the month's end** <term>huishuo: the transition between the last day of the old month and the first of the new</term>, the Sun shines only upon its outer surface <note>the side facing away from the Earth</note>, and therefore it cannot be seen at all.

<summary>This page details ominous solar phenomena—such as sunspots and eclipses—as warnings of political collapse, while also providing a scientific explanation of lunar phases as the reflection of sunlight.</summary>
<keywords>solar omens, sunspots, lunar phases, Great Yin, cultivation of virtue, political portents, Taiyin</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the astronomical discourse from the previous section. Having concluded the omens related to solar phenomena, the text now details the orbital mechanics of the Moon and its symbolic relationship to the imperial court and the Empress.</meta>

# Collected Illustrations of the Three Realms, Volume 2: Astronomy

Its movement has periods of slowness and speed. At its slowest, it travels over twelve degrees per day <gloss>at its most rapid, it travels over fourteen</gloss>
and a half degrees. From slowness, it gradually accelerates; <gloss>speed</gloss> reaches its peak and then gradually slows. In slightly over twenty-seven and a half days, one cycle of speed is completed.
Furthermore, the path of the Moon’s movement obliquely intersects the **Ecliptic** <term>Huangdao: the "Yellow Way," the apparent path of the Sun through the sky</term>. For thirteen-odd days it remains on the outer side of the Ecliptic, and for another thirteen-
odd days it remains on the inner side. At the furthest points between the outer and inner sides, it is six degrees from the Ecliptic. In twenty-seven-odd
days, one cycle of **Yin and Yang** <note>original: 陰陽一終 (yinyang yi zhong); here referring to the draconic month or the cycle of the Moon crossing the ecliptic nodes</note> is completed. **Zhang Heng** <note>78–139 CE; a renowned polymath and astronomer of the Han Dynasty</note> said: Directly opposite the Sun is a region as large as the Sun itself. Where the Sun’s light does not reach, it is called the **Dark Void** <term>Anxu: the Earth's shadow or umbra</term>.
When this Dark Void encounters the Sun, a solar eclipse occurs; when it encounters a star, that star vanishes. Current **astronomers** <term>lijia: "calendar masters" responsible for celestial calculations</term> hold that when the Moon is at its full phase and travels upon the Ecliptic,
it enters this Void. Because the Dark Void has varying depth and position, eclipses vary in their northern or southern position and their magnitude. The Moon is the **Great Yin** <term>Taiyin: the celestial essence of the feminine principle</term>;
in its pairing with the Sun, it is the symbol of the Empress. In terms of moral character, it represents the principle of punishments. In the hierarchy of the court,
it represents the feudal lords and the high ministers. Therefore, if the Sovereign is enlightened, the Moon moves according to its proper degree; if a minister usurps authority, the Moon's movement loses its proper degree.

<summary>This page describes the Moon's orbital speed and its path relative to the Ecliptic, while introducing the concept of the "Dark Void" to explain eclipses. It concludes by linking the Moon's regularity to the political stability of the Empress and high-ranking ministers.</summary>

<keywords>Moon, Ecliptic, Zhang Heng, Dark Void, Lunar Eclipse, Great Yin, Empress, Ministers, Celestial Portents</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on celestial omens. The previous page detailed the physical movement of the Moon (slowness, speed, and its intersection with the Ecliptic) and the causes of eclipses. This section transitions from the physical irregularities of the Moon to their specific political implications, before moving on to the first of the Five Planets: Jupiter.</meta>

When high officials usurp authority and the administration of military and criminal justice loses its proper principle, then the Moon's path suddenly shifts south and suddenly north. When the **Empress** <note>original: 女主 (nvzhu); referring to the Empress or Dowager</note> or her **maternal relatives** <term>waiqi: the family of the Empress or a consort, who often wielded significant political influence in the Chinese court</term> seize power, the Moon may advance or retreat erratically. 

If the Moon changes color, there will be calamity. If the Moon shines brightly during the day, treacherous and evil officials will arise together, and the ruler and ministers will compete for brilliance. If the Empress loses her virtue, the **Yin nations** <note>refers to neighboring states or vassal kingdoms, associated with "Yin" in contrast to the "Yang" of the Central Kingdom</note> will have strong armies, the Central Kingdom will suffer famine, and people throughout the world will plot subversion. If several moons are seen at once, the state will fall into chaos and perish.

### The Year Star (Jupiter)

The **Year Star** <term>Suixing: the planet Jupiter, so named because its roughly 12-year orbit was used to track the years</term> is associated with the East, the season of Spring, and the element of Wood. Among the **Five Constants** <note>the five core Confucian virtues: Benevolence, Righteousness, Propriety, Wisdom, and Trustworthiness</note> of humanity, it represents **Benevolence** <note>original: 仁 (ren)</note>. Among the **Five Matters** <note>five aspects of royal conduct: appearance, speech, vision, hearing, and thought</note>, it represents Appearance. 

If benevolence is lacking and the ruler’s appearance is unseemly, defying the seasonal ordinances of Spring and injuring the vital energy of Wood, then punishment is manifested through the Year Star. The acceleration or deceleration of the Year Star determines the fate of a nation based on the **lunar mansion** <term>xiu: one of the 28 divisions of the sky through which the planets pass</term> in which it resides. Wherever the star dwells for a long duration, that state possesses deep virtue; the five grains will be abundant and prosperous, and that state must not be attacked. 

The position directly opposite the star is known as the "Clash." In that direction, the year will bring calamity. If the Year Star is tranquil and moves according to its proper degree, it is an auspicious sign. If its advancement or regression loses its sequence, the corresponding state will suffer disaster, and one must not initiate great affairs or deploy the military. 

It is also said: The Year Star is the image of the Sovereign. Its color should be bright, clear, and lustrous, which signifies that the ruler's virtue is in harmony with the cosmos. It is further said: If its advancement and retreat follow the proper degree, then treacherous and evil...

<summary>This page describes how irregularities in the Moon's appearance and path signal the overreach of officials or the Empress, and introduces Jupiter (the Year Star) as a celestial reflection of the ruler's benevolence and the health of the state.</summary>
<keywords>Moon omens, Empress, Jupiter, Year Star, Five Constants, Benevolence, Wood element, military justice, political astrology</keywords>

If its shimmering light changes color or its movements become chaotic, the ruler will lack blessings. It is also said that this star governs the **Grand Minister of Agriculture** <term>Dasinong: a high-ranking official responsible for state finances and grain stores</term>. Furthermore, it rules over the regions of **Qi and Wu** <note>ancient states located in modern-day Shandong, Jiangsu, and Zhejiang</note> and oversees the blessings of the world's feudal lords and sovereigns.

It is also said: The Year Star governs the years and the **Five Grains** <term>Wugu: the five staple crops of ancient China, typically rice, two types of millet, wheat, and beans</term>. It is desirable for it to remain steady during the Spring; if it moves erratically, agriculture will be neglected. If the star appears red and "horned" <note>referring to visible spikes or rays of light</note>, that nation will prosper. If it appears yellow and deep, its fields will enjoy a **Bountiful Harvest** <note>original: 大穰 (daring)</note>.

**Zhang Heng** <note>a renowned astronomer and mathematician of the Han Dynasty, 78–139 CE</note> stated: "The Year Star is the essence of the East and the son of the **Azure Emperor** <term>Cangdi: the deity governing the East and the season of Spring</term>." Its alternative names include **Coordination Summoner** <note>original: 協招 (Xie Zhao)</note>, **Double Splendor** <note>original: 重華 (Chong Hua)</note>, **Response Star** <note>original: 應星 (Ying Xing)</note>, and **Chronicle Star** <note>original: 紀星 (Ji Xing)</note>.

**Jin Zhuo** <note>a historian and commentator from the Jin Dynasty</note> said: "When the **Great Year** <term>Tai Sui: an imaginary celestial body that mirrors Jupiter's orbit, used for calendrical and astrological calculations</term> is in one of the **Four Middle Months** <note>the peaks of the four seasons: the second month of Spring, Summer, Autumn, and Winter</note>, the planet moves through three **Lunar Mansions** <term>xiu: the 28 divisions of the sky through which the moon and planets pass</term>. When the Great Year is in the **Four First or Four Last Months** <note>the beginning or end of each season</note>, it moves through two mansions. Thus, [two times eight equals sixteen, and three times four equals twelve], and by moving through these twenty-eight mansions, it completes its cycle around the heavens in twelve years."

### The Sparkling Delusion (Mars)

The **Sparkling Delusion** <term>Yinghuo: the planet Mars, so named because its reddish color and retrograding motion seemed to "shimmer" and "delude" observers</term> represents the South, the season of Summer, and the element of Fire. It corresponds to the virtue of **Propriety** <note>original: 禮 (li)</note> and the sense of **Vision**. When propriety is deficient or lost, or when the commands of Summer are violated and the **Fire Qi** <note>the vital energy associated with heat, growth, and the heart</note> is harmed, then punishment is manifested through the appearance of Mars.

Mars acts as a celestial messenger; its movements are without constant rule. When it appears, there is war; when it retreats, the armies disperse. It issues fates according to the Lunar Mansions it occupies. If it lingers in a particular region's celestial station for a long time, that state will suffer calamity. It brings about chaos, rebellion, illness, death, warfare, and famine.

It is said that the **Sparkling Delusion** <term>Yinghuo: the planet Mars</term> usually enters the **Supreme Palace Enclosure** <term>Taiwei: the celestial court of the Emperor</term> in the tenth month to pay its respects. Having received its mandates, it departs to travel through the lunar mansions, where it monitors the unprincipled and brings omens of <unclear>misfortune</unclear>. 

Its arrival is irregular. It completes one full cycle of the heavens every two years.

The **Exorcist Star** <term>Zhenxing: the planet Saturn, also known as the Filling Star</term> represents the Center and is associated with the "Wu" days <note>days in the ten-day cycle associated with the element of Earth</note>. It corresponds to the planet of Late Summer, the element of **Earth**, and the virtue of **Trust** <note>the Five Virtues are Benevolence, Righteousness, Propriety, Wisdom, and Trust/Integrity</note>. Its essence is Thought and its organ is the Heart. Just as Trust is the foundation of the other four virtues, Thought is the master of speech, sight, and hearing. 

Therefore, when the other four planets lose their stability, the Exorcist Star moves to correct them. Its movements can be "Overflowing" and swift, signifying that the Earth itself is not at peace. If its movement is "Retracted," armies will fail to return from campaign. In the constellation where it resides, the corresponding nation will either lose or gain territory, and the women of the palace will find favor. One must not ignore its position; if it leaves prematurely, there will be calamity in the land, such as "womanly sorrows" <note>political instability involving the Empress or her clan</note>.

If it lingers in a constellation for a long time, the blessings for that nation are profound; if it passes through quickly, the blessings are shallow. If it strays from its proper sequence and moves forward two or three constellations, this is called "Overflowing." This portends that the ruler's decrees will not be successfully carried out; if this does not occur, then great floods will come. If it falls behind its sequence, this is called "Retraction," signaling that the Empress will be distressed. In such a year, the planet will not return to its proper path; if this does not happen, then the "sky will crack and the earth will shake" <note>a traditional metaphor for catastrophic earthquakes and total political collapse</note>.

Furthermore, the Exorcist Star embodies the virtue of the **Yellow Emperor** <note>Huangdi: the legendary ancestor of the Chinese people and symbol of Earthly power</note> and serves as the celestial image of the Empress. It governs the depth of imperial virtue and the critical turning points of a dynasty's survival or extinction. It specifically monitors the transgressions of the Empress of the world. ### The Grand White (Venus)

The **Grand White** <term>Taibai: the planet Venus</term> represents the direction of the West, the season of Autumn, and the element of **Metal**. It corresponds to the virtue of **Righteousness** and the faculty of **Speech**. 

When Righteousness is compromised or speech becomes untrustworthy, it defies the seasonal commands of Autumn and harms the Essence of Metal; consequently, celestial punishments will manifest. 

The advances and retreats of the Grand White are used to observe and predict military matters. Its height in the sky, its speed, its stillness or agitation, and whether it is visible or hidden—all of these mirror the strength and state of the army. If military actions align with these celestial signs, there is good fortune; if they run contrary, there is disaster. 

When it appears in the West and deviates from its proper path, the **foreign tribes** <note>literally "Yi and Di," referring to non-Han peoples residing on the borders of ancient China</note> will be defeated. When it appears in the East and deviates from its path, the **Central Kingdom** <note>China</note> will suffer defeat. 

If it has not yet completed its expected cycle but stays in the sky for an excessive number of days, sickness will strike the opposing nation. 

It is also said: "When the Grand White appears high in the sky, invading deep into enemy territory is auspicious, while a shallow campaign is unlucky. When it appears low, a shallow campaign is auspicious, and a deep invasion is unlucky." 

If the planet is large when it first appears and later becomes small, the army will be weak; if it is small at first and later grows large, the army will be strong. Whosoever follows the direction indicated by the rays and "spikes" <note>the shimmering points of light seen around a bright planet</note> of the Grand White shall find good fortune; to act against that direction brings great calamity. 

It is said that when the Grand White resides in the southern region...

### San Cai Tu Hui <note>Collected Illustrations of the Three Realms</note>, Volume 3: Astronomy

When the Sun is in the south and the **Grand White** <term>Taibai: the planet Venus</term> resides to its north, or when the Sun is in the north and the Grand White also resides to its north, it is termed "Overextending." In such times, the ruler will be restless. Regarding military affairs: advancing brings good fortune, while retreating brings disaster. 

When the Sun is in the south and the Grand White resides to its north <note>The text likely implies a relative lag here</note>, or when the Sun is in the north and the Grand White resides to its south, it is termed "Contracting." The ruler will be filled with worry. In military affairs: retreating brings good fortune, while advancing brings disaster. 

If the planet **crosses the sky** <note>original: *jingtian* — a rare phenomenon where Venus is visible during the day, considered a grave omen</note>, the world will fall into primitive chaos and the people must be "born again" <note>this implies a total upheaval or a change in dynasty</note>. This signifies the severing of order and the displacement of the populace. If it competes in brightness with the Sun during the day, strong nations will be weakened, small nations will grow powerful, and the Empress will flourish. 

It is also said that the Grand White governs the high ministers; its celestial title is the "Supreme Duke." The status and actions of the **Great Minister of War** <note>Da Sima: one of the highest military offices in ancient China</note> are to be carefully observed through the movements of this star. 

**Zhang Heng** <note>78–139 CE, a famous Chinese polymath and astronomer</note> said: "The Grand White is the son of the **White Emperor** <note>the deity presiding over the West and the element of Metal</note>." It is also known as:

*   the **Heavenly Vastness**
*   the **Order Star**
*   the **Cunning Star**
*   the **Majesty Star**
*   the **Great Clamor**
*   the **Great Brightness**

**Jin Zhuo** <note>a 3rd-century scholar and commentator</note> stated: "Starting on the *Jiayin* day <note>the 51st day of the 60-day cycle</note> of the first lunar month, the star appears in the East alongside **Mars** <term>Yinghuo: the Shimmering Deception</term> for two hundred and forty days before setting. It remains hidden for forty days, then reappears in the West for another two hundred and forty days."

The astronomer **Zhang Heng** <note>78–139 CE; a renowned Han dynasty polymath who improved the armillary sphere and defined celestial motions</note> states that **Mercury** <term>Chenxing: the "Hour Star," associated with the north</term> is also known as the **Hook Star** <note>original: *Gouxing*</note>, the **Whispering Star** <note>original: *Qixing*</note>, and the **Watching Star** <note>original: *Sixing*</note>.

The commentator **Jin Zhuo** <note>a 3rd-century scholar known for his detailed annotations on historical texts</note> explains its seasonal movements:

In the second month, during the **Vernal Equinox**, the star is seen in the mansions of **Kui** and **Lou** <note>the "Astral Bridge" and "Bond" mansions, located in Andromeda and Aries</note>. In the fifth month, during the **Summer Solstice**, it appears in the **Eastern Well** <note>the mansion of *Dongjing* in Gemini</note>. In the eighth month, during the **Autumnal Equinox**, it is visible in the mansions of **Jue** and **Kang** <note>the "Horn" and "Neck" mansions in Virgo and Libra</note>. In the eleventh month, during the **Winter Solstice**, it appears in the **Ox** mansion <note>the mansion of *Niu* in Capricorn</note>.

When the star emerges, it does so at the positions of *Chen* or *Xu* <note>the 5th and 11th Terrestrial Branches, representing specific compass directions and times</note>, and it enters at the positions of *Chou* or *Wei* <note>the 2nd and 8th Terrestrial Branches</note>. It remains visible for twenty days before disappearing. One should observe it in the **East** during the dawn and in the **West** during the dusk. It completes one full circuit of the heavens every year.

### General Discussion on the Motion of the Seven Governors

Ever since the beginning of **Primal Chaos** <note>original: *Hunyuan*; the primordial state of the universe before the separation of heaven and earth</note>, the **Seven Governors** <term>Qi Zheng: the Sun, the Moon, and the five visible planets (Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn)</term> have been in motion. The order of the years has shifted and changed; there are celestial omens that can be divined, and there are mathematical cycles that can be calculated.

From this, the science of the calendar and celestial numbers was born. Now, the Sun, Moon, and stars are those things that have physical form and move in the heavens above. The **Four Seasons** and the **Six Qi** <term>Six Qi: the six atmospheric or energetic influences—wind, cold, heat, moisture, dryness, and fire—that govern the climate</term> are those things that lack physical form and move on the earth below. That which has form and that which is formless do not appear to be companions; however, these two are actually bound together in a regulatory relationship to complete the work of the year.

For the Sun completes its circuit through the **Zodiacal Stations** <note>original: *ci*; the twelve divisions of the Chinese zodiac</note>, the Moon completes its cycle through its periods, and the stars revolve across the heavens—this is the motion of that which has form above, constituting the year. Five days make a **Pentad** <note>original: *hou*; a five-day unit of the traditional calendar</note>, three pentads make a **Solar Term** <note>original: *qi*; the 24 fifteen-day divisions of the year</note>, six solar terms make a season, and four seasons make a year—this is the motion of that which is formless below, also constituting the year.

At the beginning of Primal Chaos, the Sun and Moon were aligned like **joined jade discs**, and the five planets were aligned like a **string of pearls** <note>original: *hebi lianzhu*; a rare and highly auspicious planetary alignment signifying cosmic harmony</note>. From the moment they began their motion until now, they have never met again in such a perfect conjunction. Why is this? It is because the movements of the Seven Governors vary in speed; therefore, their re-conjunction is extremely difficult. The Sun's...

<meta>Catchword: ...movement (日之)</meta>

---

<summary>This page provides a theoretical foundation for Chinese astronomy, explaining the duality between visible celestial motions (the Sun, Moon, and planets) and the invisible seasonal cycles (the Four Seasons and Six Qi). It emphasizes that while these two systems seem separate, they are mathematically and physically linked to create the calendar.</summary>

<keywords>Seven Governors, Primal Chaos, celestial mechanics, Six Qi, Solar Terms, planetary alignment, joined jade discs, string of pearls</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion on the specific orbital paths of the inner planets, this page expands the scope to compare the orbital periods of all "Seven Governors" and addresses a common historical misconception regarding planetary alignments found in the Han Dynasty records.</meta>

The Sun travels through the heavens, completing one circuit in a single year. The Moon travels through the heavens, completing one circuit in a single month. The cycle of **Jupiter** <term>Sui Xing: the 'Year Star'; its approximately twelve-year orbit serves as the basis for the twelve-branch cycle of the Chinese zodiac</term> usually takes twelve years. <gloss>Those in the world who believe the common word for "year" is derived from Jupiter's cycle will find the root of that argument here.</gloss> The cycle of **Saturn** <term>Zhen Xing: the 'Quelling Star'; historically associated with the element of Earth and the 28 Lunar Mansions</term> takes twenty-eight years.

The cycle of **Mars** <note>original: *Yan Huo*, likely a transcription error for *Ying Huo*, the "Sparkling Delusion," named for its erratic movement and reddish hue</note> takes two years. Only **Venus** <term>Tai Bai: "Great White"</term> and **Mercury** <term>Chen Xing: "Hour Star"</term> travel in close proximity to the Sun. Sometimes they are fast and move ahead of the Sun; sometimes they are slow and fall behind the Sun. When they are fast and move ahead, they are seen in the west at dusk. When they are slow and fall behind, they are seen in the east at dawn. In essence, their journey across the heavens is nearly identical to the Sun's; therefore, they also complete one circuit of the heavens per year. It is precisely because the movements of these **Seven Governors** are inconsistent in this manner that they are so difficult to bring into alignment. 

Those in the world who read the *History of the Han* see the discussion regarding the precision of the **Taichu Calendar** <note>original: *Taichu Li*; the "Grand Inception" calendar commissioned by Emperor Wu of Han in 104 BCE to reform timekeeping</note>, which describes the Sun and Moon as **Joined Jade Discs** <term>He Bi: a metaphorical term for a perfect syzygy or conjunction</term> and the five planets as **Strung Pearls** <term>Lian Zhu: a rare planetary alignment where the planets appear in a line like a necklace</term>. Consequently, they claim that the five planets met in the first year of the **Taichu** era. 

They do not realize, however, that this passage is actually discussing the mathematical perfection of the **Taichu Calendar**. If one calculates backward to the very beginning of **Primal Chaos** <note>original: *Hunyuan*; the primordial state of the universe before time and form were divided</note>, where the precision of the celestial numbers is so absolute that no fractional remainders exist, only then could such a statement be made. In the actual first year of the **Taichu** era, the planets never truly aligned in such a fashion...

<summary>This page outlines the varying orbital periods of the five visible planets and explains the "fast" and "slow" appearances of Venus and Mercury. It also clarifies that the famous "pearl-string" alignment mentioned in Han Dynasty records was a mathematical idealization from the start of the calendar's cycle, rather than an observed event in 104 BCE.</summary>

<keywords>Seven Governors, Jupiter, Saturn, Mars, Venus, Mercury, Taichu Calendar, planetary alignment, Joined Jade Discs, Strung Pearls, Primal Chaos</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion regarding the orbital periods of the Seven Governors (the Sun, Moon, and five visible planets), this page addresses the historical and mathematical feasibility of the famous planetary alignment recorded at the beginning of the Han Dynasty. The author uses the slow orbital speed of Saturn to challenge the traditional accounts of the Taichu calendar's starting point.</meta>

The phenomena of "joining like a pair of jade discs" and "aligning like a string of pearls" are described in such a way—but why is this said? The meeting of the **Five Planets** <term>the five visible planets: Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</term> always follows the **Quelling Star** <term>Zhen Xing: Saturn</term>. Regarding the movements of the five planets, the Quelling Star is the slowest; therefore, the other planets must wait for it to arrive in a specific region of the sky for a conjunction to occur. 

If we examine this using astronomical calculations, in the first year of the reign of **Emperor Gaozu of Han** <note>reigned 206–195 BCE; the founder of the Han Dynasty</note>, the five planets gathered in the **Eastern Well** <term>Dongjing: a lunar mansion located in the modern constellation of Gemini</term>, which corresponds to the station of the **Quail’s Head** <term>Chunshou: one of the twelve divisions of the Chinese zodiac</term>. 

From the first year of Emperor Gaozu to the first year of the **Taichu** era <note>104 BCE, when the landmark Taichu calendar reform occurred</note>, a total of one hundred and eleven years passed. The Quelling Star takes twenty-eight years to complete one full circuit of the heavens. At that time, the Quelling Star's journey through the sky would have already completed three full cycles, and it would have traveled more than half of its fourth cycle, passing through a total of eight stations. 

Since it had advanced into the station of the **Dark Void** <term>Xuanxiao: the zodiacal division containing the constellation Aquarius</term>, how could it be possible for the Sun and Moon to "join like jade discs" and the five planets to "align like a string of pearls" beginning at the start of the **Ox** <term>Qianniu: a lunar mansion in the constellation Capricornus</term> constellation? <margin>The Ox is located within the station of the **Star Record** <term>Xingji: the zodiacal division associated with the winter solstice</term>.</margin>

<summary>The author uses Saturn's 28-year orbital period to argue that the planetary alignment claimed by the Taichu calendar's proponents was mathematically impossible based on the earlier alignment recorded at the Han Dynasty's founding.</summary>
<keywords>Saturn, Quelling Star, Emperor Gaozu, Taichu Calendar, planetary alignment, Eastern Well, Quail's Head, Dark Void, Ox constellation, Star Record</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of astronomical calculations, transitioning from the general determination of the year to the specific divisions of the celestial sphere used to track the sun and moon.</meta>

The Twelve Stations are recorded to determine the transition from the old moon to the new moon <note>original: 晦朔; 'hui' is the last day of the lunar month and 'shuo' is the first day (the new moon)</note>, through which the cycle of the year is completed.

The commentary states: The **Twelve Stations** <term>shier ci; the twelve divisions of the ecliptic through which the sun passes</term> are also known as the **Twelve Branches** <term>shier chen</term>; they represent the points where the sun and the moon meet. The *Treatise of the Jin Dynasty* <note>original: 晉志; referencing the astronomical chapters in the official History of the Jin</note> mentions that the historian Ban Gu utilized the Twelve Stations from the *Triple Concordance Calendar* and paired them with the **Twelve Terrestrial Fields** <term>shier ye; the ancient system of "Field Allocation" (fenye) where regions on Earth correspond to specific constellations</term>. His account is the most comprehensive. There are also the interpretations found in Fei Zhi's work on the *Book of Changes* and Cai Yong's *Commentary on the Monthly Ordinances*, though their records of chronological precedence vary. Chen Zhuo, the Grand Astrologer of the Wei Dynasty, further specified the degrees of the **Lunar Mansions** <term>xiu; the 28 constellations of the Chinese zodiac</term> that fall within these divisions. These are now appended and organized in order:

From the 12th degree of the **Chariot** mansion <note>original: 軫; Zhen</note> to the 4th degree of the **Foundation** mansion <note>original: 氐; Di</note> is the station of **Longevity Star** <term>Shouxing</term>. In the cycle of the earthly branches, it corresponds to **Chen**. It is the celestial field of the State of Zheng and belongs to **Yan Province**.

From the 5th degree of the **Foundation** mansion to the 9th degree of the **Tail** mansion <note>original: 尾; Wei</note> is the station of **Great Fire** <term>Dahuo</term>. In the cycle of the branches, it corresponds to **Mao**. It is the celestial field of the State of Song and belongs to **Yu Province**.

From the 10th degree of the **Tail** mansion to the 11th degree of the **Southern Dipper** mansion <note>original: 南斗; Nandou</note> is the station of **Split Wood** <term>Ximu</term>. In the cycle of the branches, it corresponds to **Yin**. It is the celestial field of the State of Yan and belongs to **You Province**.

From the 12th degree of the **Southern Dipper** mansion to the 7th degree of the **Maiden** mansion <note>original: 須女; Xünü</note> is the station of **Star Record** <term>Xingji</term>. In the cycle of the branches, it corresponds to **Chou**. It is the celestial field of the States of Wu and Yue and belongs to **Yang Province**.

From the 8th degree of the **Maiden** mansion to the 15th degree of the **Rooftop** mansion <note>original: 危; Wei</note> is the station of **Dark Void** <term>Xuanxiao</term>. In the cycle of the branches...

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of celestial mansions and their geographical correlations. The previous page ended mid-sentence while listing the mansions associated with the branch Shen. This page shifts from specific regional allocations to a broader historical overview of how astronomers calculated the "Precession of the Equinoxes"—the gradual shift of stars relative to the solar year.</meta>

### Collected Illustrations of the Three Realms, Volume 3: Astronomy

Regarding the theories of various schools concerning the degrees of movement for the **Twenty-Eight Mansions** <term>the 28 constellations of the Chinese zodiac used to track the moon's progress</term>, there are mutual discrepancies. This is because the movement of the heavens and the shifts of the **Seven Luminaries** <term>the Sun, Moon, and the five visible planets: Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</term> are not perfectly uniform. Therefore, calendar makers throughout history established the method of **Precession of the Equinoxes** <note>original: 歲差 (suicha); the discovery that the positions of the stars slowly change over long periods relative to the equinoxes</note>.

For instance, in the time of **Emperor Yao** <note>a legendary sage-king of ancient China, traditionally dated to the 3rd millennium BCE</note>, the Winter Solstice was positioned at 1 degree of the **Void** mansion; the Summer Solstice was at 14 degrees of the **Willow** mansion; the Spring Equinox was at 12 degrees of the **Stomach** mansion; and the Autumn Equinox was at 10 degrees of the **Root** mansion.

By the time of the **Great Extension Calendar** <note>original: 大衍曆 (Dayan Li); a highly influential calendar system produced in 727 CE</note> during the Kaiyuan era of the Tang Dynasty, the Winter Solstice sun was at 10 degrees of the **Dipper**; the Summer Solstice was at 10 degrees of the **Well**; the Spring Equinox was at 7 degrees of the **Stride**; and the Autumn Equinox was at 14 degrees of the **Chariot**.

Looking at it from this perspective, the astronomical calculations for the Seven Luminaries shifted this much over the two thousand years from the time of Yao to the Tang Dynasty. It is no wonder that the theories of those who discussed the heavens throughout the ages differed from one another.

However, **Yu Xi** of the Jin Dynasty estimated that the sun retreated one degree every fifty years, which proved to be too great an adjustment. **He Chengtian** of the Liu Song Dynasty then doubled that number, estimating a retreat of one degree every hundred years, which was an underestimation. **Liu Zhuo** <note>an astronomer of the Sui Dynasty known for his "Huangji Calendar"</note> then took the middle ground between these two schools, setting the shift at one degree every seventy-five years, which was much closer to the truth. Later, the Tang monk **Yixing** further refined the *Great Extension Calendar*, calculating the shift at eighty-three...

<summary>This page explains the historical development of the concept of "Precession of the Equinoxes" in Chinese astronomy, comparing the solar positions during the time of Emperor Yao to the Tang Dynasty. It highlights the shifting calculations of prominent astronomers like Yu Xi, He Chengtian, and Liu Zhuo in their attempts to measure this celestial drift.</summary>
<keywords>Twenty-Eight Mansions, Seven Luminaries, Precession of the Equinoxes (Suicha), Emperor Yao, Great Extension Calendar (Dayan Li), Yu Xi, He Chengtian, Liu Zhuo, Yixing, Tang Dynasty, Chinese Astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the "Precession of the Equinoxes" (the gradual shift of stars relative to the solar year). The previous page ended while listing the positions of the equinoxes during the Tang Dynasty. This page addresses the historical variations in calculating this shift and explains the author's choice of source material for the accompanying celestial chart.</meta>

...[every seventy-five] years, shifting by one degree. Since ancient times, those who have managed the calendars have generally followed this method. However, **Guo Shoujing** <note>a brilliant astronomer and engineer of the Yuan Dynasty (1231–1316 CE) who developed the highly accurate Shoushi Calendar</note> of the Yuan Dynasty stated that the shift was one degree every sixty-six years. 

Commentators regarded this as the height of precision and accuracy. Yet, why is it that in the two hundred years leading up to the present, the calculations performed by the **Bureau Officials** <term>officials of the Imperial Astronomical Bureau responsible for maintaining the official calendar</term> have again frequently failed to match the actual positions of the stars? If this is the case, how can the degrees of movement for the various constellations be restricted to a single, fixed theory?

In my engraving of this chart, I have followed only the sequence of degrees for the **Twenty-Eight Mansions** as recorded by **Zheng Qiao** <note>a Southern Song Dynasty scholar (1104–1162 CE) known for his encyclopedic work, the *Tongzhi* (Comprehensive Treatise)</note> of the Song Dynasty. I have not dared to fully record all the various and conflicting theories. My hope is that those in future generations who are dedicated to **astronomical calculation** <term>original: 推步 (tuibu); literally "calculating the steps" of celestial bodies to predict their future positions</term> will verify these figures against the sky as time passes, seeking to bring them into harmony with the heavens. That will be sufficient.

<summary>This page discusses the historical difficulty of accurately calculating the Precession of the Equinoxes, noting that even "precise" calculations by masters like Guo Shoujing eventually diverged from observation. The author explains he based his celestial chart on the work of Zheng Qiao, leaving future corrections to dedicated astronomers.</summary>
<keywords>Guo Shoujing, Zheng Qiao, Precession of the Equinoxes, Twenty-Eight Mansions, astronomical calculation, Imperial Astronomical Bureau, Song Dynasty, Yuan Dynasty</keywords>

<note>Historical Note: In traditional Chinese astronomy, a "degree" (du) was defined by the sun's daily movement, resulting in 365.25 degrees in a full circle. The "minutes" (fen) shown here, particularly for the Shoushi calendar, are decimal parts of 100 rather than the Western 60.</note>

<term>Tongtian Calendar (Universal Heaven)</term> <note>promulgated in 1199 CE during the Southern Song Dynasty.</note>
<term>Kaixi Calendar (Opened Joy)</term> <note>introduced in 1205 CE to correct errors in the Tongtian system.</note>
<term>Huitian Calendar (Meeting Heaven)</term> <note>a late 13th-century system used during the transition to the Yuan Dynasty.</note>
<term>Shoushi Calendar (Season Granting)</term> <note>the most advanced calendar of pre-modern China, created by Guo Shoujing in 1281 CE using sophisticated spherical trigonometry.</note>

<summary>This page presents a comparative table of the width (in degrees) of the lunar mansions as calculated by four different historical Chinese calendars. It highlights the evolution of astronomical precision, culminating in the highly accurate decimal-based measurements of the Shoushi Calendar.</summary>
<keywords>Lunar Mansions, Ecliptic, Shoushi Calendar, Tongtian Calendar, Kaixi Calendar, Huitian Calendar, Chinese astronomy, degrees and minutes</keywords>

### Collected Illustrations of the Three Realms, Volume 21: Astronomy

| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Zhou** | Willow: 4 degrees <gloss>Willow: 5 degrees, 96/96 minutes; Willow: 3 degrees, 37 minutes, 34.28 seconds, 80 seconds</gloss> | | Entering the Horse | Station of the Quail Fire |
| **Chu** | Extended Net: 1 degree <gloss>62 minutes, 52 minutes, 57 minutes, 36 minutes; 44.44 seconds, 79.6 seconds</gloss> | | Entering the Snake | Station of the Quail Tail |
| **Zheng** | Chariot: 1 degree <gloss>65 minutes, 56 minutes, 50 minutes, 7 minutes; 0 seconds, 24 seconds, 26.97 seconds</gloss> | | Entering the Dragon | Station of the Longevity Star |

->The various divisions above are based on the celestial equator, adjusted by axial precession <term>Precession (Sui-cha) is the slow shift in the orientation of Earth's axis, which changes the perceived position of the stars over centuries</term> over four years into ecliptic coordinates.<-

The field allotments <note>the traditional Chinese system of mapping earthly provinces to specific constellations</note>, mansion degrees, and palace sequences in the preceding chart are all based on the records found in Zheng Qiao’s *Comprehensive History* <note>original: "Tongzhi," a massive 12th-century encyclopedia</note>. However, because of the precession of the equinoxes, these figures inevitably differ from those found in the *Pervading Heaven*, *Opening Greatness*, *Meeting Heaven*, and *Season-Granting* calendars <note>These are specific historical Chinese calendars: the Tongtian, Kaixi, Huitian, and the famous Shoushi calendar of 1281</note>. They are now listed here in detail for easy reference and inspection.

<summary>This page presents a technical table correlating five ancient Chinese states with specific astronomical coordinates (degrees in Lunar Mansions) and their corresponding positions in the Chinese zodiac. It notes the necessity of updating these coordinates from older sources like the Tongzhi to account for the precession of the equinoxes.</summary>
